<!-- thm-1: body_md -->
\cite{Ahl63}\label{thm:Ahl}
A planar set is the image of a line segment under a quasiconformal self-map of the plane if and only if it is a bounded turning arc.
<!-- end thm-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:semigeodesic}
We say that a metric tree $T$ is \emph{semi-geodesic} if 
\begin{enumerate}
\item there exists $C\geq 1$ such that for every two points $x,y \in T$ there exists an arc that joins them of length at most $C d(x,y)$,
\item for each $x\in T\setminus \mathcal{L}(T)$ there exists a line segment $I\subset T$ that contains $x$ in its interior.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- quest-1: body_md -->
\label{quest1}
Is every planar quasiconformal tree $T$ quasiconformally homeomorphic to a semi-geodesic $T'$?
<!-- end quest-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:unifsep}
A metric tree $(T,d)$ is said to have \textit{uniformly (relatively) separated branch points} if there exists $C\geq 1$ with the following property. If $p,q\in T$ are distinct branch points, and if $B_{T}(p)$ (resp.~$B_{T}(q)$) is the third largest component of $T\setminus \{p\}$ (resp.~$T\setminus\{q\}$) in diameter, then
$$d(p,q)\geq C^{-1}\min\{\operatorname{diam}{B_{T}(p)},\operatorname{diam}{B_{T}(q)}\}.$$
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- thm-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:main thm}
If $T$ is a planar quasiconformal tree with uniform branch separation and if $\varepsilon>0$, then there exists a quasiconformal map $f:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ such that $f(T)$ is a semi-geodesic tree with $\dim_H{f(T)}<1+\epsilon$.
<!-- end thm-2 -->
<!-- cor-1: body_md -->
If $T\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ is a quasiconformal tree with uniform branch separation, then $\operatorname{GQC^{2}dim}(T)=1$.
<!-- end cor-1 -->
<!-- thm-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:Schoenflies-arcs}
Let $I \subset \mathbb{R}^2$ be a line segment and let $f:I\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be an embedding.
\begin{enumerate}
                                                                         
\item\cite{Ahl63,BA56} If $f$ is quasisymmetric, then there exists a quasiconformal extension $F:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$.
\item\cite{Tukia} If $f$ is bi-Lipschitz, then there exists a bi-Lipschitz extension $F:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end thm-3 -->
<!-- thm-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:Schoenflies-trees}
Let $T$ be a planar quasiconformal tree and let $f: T \to \mathbb{R}^2$ be a branch-orientation-preserving embedding. 
\begin{enumerate}
\item If $f$ is quasisymmetric, then there exists a quasiconformal orientation preserving extension $F:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$.
\item If $f$ is bi-Lipschitz, then there exists a bi-Lipschitz orientation preserving extension $F:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$.
\end{enumerate}
Conversely, if $F:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ is an orientation preserving homeomorphism and if $T\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ is a tree, then $F|T$ is branch-orientation-preserving.
<!-- end thm-4 -->
<!-- thm-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:Vicsek}
There exists a quadratic UQR map $f$ of $\mathbb{S}^2$ whose Julia set is $\mathbb{V}$.
<!-- end thm-5 -->
<!-- lem-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:Jordan domain prerequisite}
Let $O\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ be a Jordan domain with boundary $\Gamma$. Let $x\in \Gamma$ and $r>0$ such that $\partial B(x,r)\cap O\neq\emptyset$. Let $p,q\in \partial B(x,r)\cap \Gamma$ such that $x\in \Gamma(p,q)\subset B(x,r)$. Denote by $W_1,W_2$ the connected components of $B(x,r)\setminus\Gamma[p,q]$. There exist arcs $\gamma_1,\gamma_2$ with one common endpoint $p'\in \Gamma[p,x)$, the other common endpoint $q'\in \Gamma[q,x)$ such that $\gamma_1\setminus\{p',q'\}\subset W_1\cap O$ and $\gamma_2\setminus\{p',q'\}\subset W_2\cap (\mathbb{R}^2\setminus \overline{O})$.
<!-- end lem-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Let $p,q,B(x,r)$ as in the statement. Let $h:\mathbb{R}^2\to \mathbb{R}^2$ be a homeomorphism that sends $\Gamma[p,q]$ to a line segment $l$. Let $r'>0$ such that $B(h(x),r')\subset h(B(x,r))$. Then $B(h(x),r')\setminus l$ has two connected components that lie inside $h(B(x,r))$ and do not intersect $h(\Gamma)$ other than $l$. By taking inverse images and by the fact that $h$ is a homeomorphism the result follows.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:bounded turning}
A set $X\subset \mathbb{R}^n$ is of $C$-\textit{bounded turning}, for some $C\geq 1$, if for any $x,y\in X$ there exists a continuum $K\subset X$ containing $x,y$ such that $\operatorname{diam} K\leq C|x-y|$.
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{def:John domain}
A domain $\Omega\subset \mathbb{R}^n$ is $C$-\textit{John} if there exists a constant $C\geq 1$ such that for all $x,y\in\Omega$ there exists an arc $\gamma\subset \Omega$ connecting $x$ to $y$ and for all $z\in\gamma[x,y]$
\begin{align}\label{eq:John}
\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\Omega)\geq C^{-1}\min\{\ell(\gamma[z,x]),\ell(\gamma[z,y])\}.
\end{align}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- lem-2: body_md -->
\label{lem: Nakki vaisalla}
Let $X\subset \overline{\mathbb{R}^2}$ be a non-degenerate continuum. Then, $X$ is $C$-bounded turning if and only if $\overline{\mathbb{R}^2}\setminus X$ is a $C$-John domain.
<!-- end lem-2 -->
<!-- lem-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:qc removable}
Let $X\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ be a closed set and let $F:\mathbb{R}^2\to\mathbb{R}^2$ be a homeomorphism. If $F|\mathbb{R}^2\setminus X$ is quasiconformal and $\mathbb{R}^2\setminus X$ is a John domain, then $F$ is quasiconformal.
<!-- end lem-3 -->
<!-- rem-1: body_md -->
If $f:(X,d_X)\to (Y,d_Y)$ is an $(L,\lambda)$-quasi-similarity, then it is $L^2$-quasiconformal and $K$-bi-Lipschitz with $K=\max\{\lambda L,\lambda/L\}$. Moreover, if $f$ is $L$-bi-Lipschitz, then $f$ is $\eta(L^2t)$-quasisymmetric.
<!-- end rem-1 -->
<!-- lem-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:Tukia}
Let $D,D'\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ be Jordan domains and let $h:\partial D\to\partial D'$ be an $(s,L)$-quasi-similarity. Then $h$ admits an extension $ H:\overline{D}\to\overline{D'}$ which is an $(s,L')$-quasi-similarity, where $L'$ depends only on $L$. The analogous statement holds for the unbounded complementary components of Jordan curves.
<!-- end lem-4 -->
<!-- lem-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:properties of thickening}
Let $S\subset\mathbb{R}^2$ be a bounded set and let $\delta>0$.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item The set $\partial\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$ consists of finitely many mutually disjoint Jordan curves, each of which is a subset of $\mathcal{G}_\delta$. Moreover, there exists a Jordan domain $\Omega$ such that $\partial\Omega\subset \partial\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$ and $S \subset \Omega$.
\item For all $y\in \partial\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$, $\delta<\operatorname{dist}(y,S)<8\delta$. In particular, 
$$\operatorname{diam}\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)\leq \operatorname{diam} S+16\delta.$$
\item If $S'\subset S$, then $\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S')\subset\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$.
\item If $\{A_i\}_{i\in I}$ are sets in $\mathbb{R}^2$ with $S=\bigcup_{i\in I} A_i$, then $\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)=\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(A_i)$.
\item If $\delta'\in (0,\delta]$, then $\mathscr{T}_{\delta'}(S)\subset \mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$.
\item For each $z\in\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$ there exists $w\in S$ such that $|z-w| < 9\delta$ and $[z,w]\subset \mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-5 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Claims (i)-(v) follow directly from the definitions and from \cite[Lemma 2.1]{Mac99}. To show (vi), fix $z\in\mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$. Then $z$ belongs to a square $Q_1\in \Sigma_{\delta}$ that intersects a square $Q_2\in \Sigma_{4\delta}$ that intersects $S$. Let $w\in Q_{2}\cap S$. Since $Q_1\cap Q_{2}\neq\emptyset$ the squares $Q_1,Q_{2}$ share at least a side or a corner. Let 
\[Q' = Q_{2}\cup \bigcup_{\substack{Q \subset \Sigma_\delta\\ Q \cap Q_{2} \neq \emptyset}} Q.\]
Note that $Q'$ is a square, $Q'\subset \mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$, and $z,w\in Q'$. We conclude that $[z,w]\subset \mathscr{T}_{\delta}(S)$ and that $|z-w|\leq \operatorname{diam} Q'<9\delta$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lem-6: body_md -->
\label{lem: metric trees properties of BM}
Let $(T,d)$ be a metric tree.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item Let $V\subset T$ be a finite set. Two points $x, y \in T\setminus V$ lie in the same component of $T \setminus V$ if and only $T[x,y]\cap V=\emptyset$.
\item Let  $b, x_1, x_2, x_3\in T$ with $b \neq x_1, x_2, x_3$ and suppose that the sets $T[x_1, b)$, $T[x_2, b)$, $T[x_3, b)$ are pairwise disjoint. Then the points $x_1, x_2, x_3$ lie in different components of $T \setminus\{b\}$, and $b$ is a branch point of $T$.
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-6 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Claim (i) follows from \cite[Lemma 2.3(i)]{BM20}. Claim (ii) follows from \cite[Lemma 2.4]{BM20}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lem-7: body_md -->
\label{lem: maximal arcs}
Let $T\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ be a metric tree, let $b\in T$ be a branch point of valence $k\geq 3$, and let $B_1,\dots,B_k$ be the components of $T\setminus\{b\}$. Let $D,D'$ be Jordan domains that contain $b$ and intersect $B_i$ for all $i\in \{1,\dots,k\}$.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item For each $i\in \{1,\dots,k\}$ there exists $p_i\in B_i\cap \partial D$ and $q_i\in B_i\cap \partial D'$ such that $T[b,p_i)\subset D$ and $T[b,q_i)\subset D'$.
\item If $p_{i_1},\dots,p_{i_k}$ and $q_{j_1},\dots,q_{j_k}$ are the counterclockwise enumerations along $\partial D$ and $\partial D'$, respectively, and if $i_1=j_1$, then $i_l=j_l$ for all $l\in \{2,\dots,k\}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-7 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
To show (i), fix $r>0$ such that $\overline{B}(b,r)\subset D$. For each $i\in \{1,\dots,k\}$ let $y_i\in \partial D\cap B_i$ and note that  $T[b,y_i]\subset \overline{B_i}$. Let $p_i\in B_i$ be the first time that the arc $T[b,y_i]$ meets $\partial D$ as we travel from $b$ to $y_i$. It is then guaranteed that $T[b,p_i)\subset D$ since there exists a subarc of $T[b,p_i)$ lying entirely in $\overline{B}(b,r)$. The existence of $\{q_i\}$ on $\partial D'$ is similar.

To show (ii), suppose for a contradiction that $i_1=j_1$ and that $j_2=i_{l}$ for some $l\in\{3,\dots,k\}$. We start with a special case and then prove (ii) in full generality.

\emph{Case 1:} Suppose that $\overline{D}\subset D'$. By Schoenflies and Annulus theorem applying an orientation preserving homeomorphism $h$ we may assume that $b=0$, $D = B(0,1)$, and $D'=B(0,2)$. Let $\sigma_i=T[0,p_i]$, $\gamma_i=T[0,q_i]$. For any $x,y\in \partial B(0,2)$ (resp. $x,y\in \partial B(0,1)$) denote also by $P[x,y]$ (resp. $Q[x,y]$) the counterclockwise arc in $\partial B(0,2)$ (resp. in $\partial B(0,1)$) from $x$ to $y$. Write $B(0,2)=S_1\cup S_2\cup S_3$ where 
\begin{align*}
        \partial S_1&=P[q_{i_1},q_{i_l}]\cup  \gamma_{i_l} \cup \gamma_{i_1} \\
        \partial S_2&= P[q_{i_l},q_{i_2}]\cup  \gamma_{i_2} \cup \gamma_{i_l} \\
        \partial S_3&=P[q_{i_2},q_{i_1}]\cup  \gamma_k \cup \gamma_{i_2}.
\end{align*}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{maximalarcs.png}
                                 
\label{fig:maximal_arcs 1}
\end{figure}
We claim that $\sigma_{i_1}\subset S_1\cup S_3$. If not, then $\sigma_{i_1}\subset S_2$ due to the fact that $T$ is a tree, $h$ is a homeomorphism, and that $\sigma_{i_3}\subset B(0,2)$. But then $\{0\}= \gamma_{i_1}\cap \sigma_{i_1}$ which implies that $0\in T[p_{i_1},q_{i_1}]$ which is impossible since $p_{i_1},q_{i_1}\in B_{i_1}$ by Lemma \ref{lem: metric trees properties of BM}(i). Similarly, $\sigma_{i_l}\subset S_1\cup S_2$ and $\sigma_{i_2}\subset S_2\cup S_3$. Suppose that $p_{i_1}\in S_3$; if $p_{i_1}\in S_1$ similar arguments work. Since $p_{i_2}\in Q[p_{i_1},p_{i_l}]$ it follows that either $\sigma_{i_l}\cup \sigma_{i_2}\subset S_2$ or that $\sigma_{i_2}\subset S_3$ and $\sigma_{i_l}\subset S_1$. We show the former and the latter follows with similar arguments. Note that $T[0,q_{i_2}]\cap T[0,p_{i_2}]= T[0,b']$ where $b'\in B_{i_2}$ is a branch point from Lemma \ref{lem: metric trees properties of BM}(ii). Since $\sigma_{i_l}\setminus\{p_{i_l}\}\subset B(0,1)$ it follows that 
$$\emptyset\neq\sigma_{i_l}\cap (\gamma_{i_2}[b',q_{i_2}]\cup\sigma_{i_2}[b',p_{i_2}])\subset B_{i_l}\cap B_{i_2}$$
which is contradiction due to the fact that $T$ is a tree.

We work similarly if $\overline{D'}\subset D$.

\emph{Case 2:} Suppose that $\partial D\cap \partial D'\neq\emptyset$. Let $r>0$ so that $\overline{B}(b,r)\subset D\cap D'$. For each $t\in\{1,\dots,k\}$ let $p_{i_t}'$ (resp. $q_{j_t}'$) be the first time we meet $\partial B(b,r)$ as we travel from $b$ to $p_{i_t}$ (resp. to $q_{j_t}$). By Case 1 we have the counterclockwise enumerations $p_{i_1}',p_{i_2}',\dots,p_{i_l}'$ and $q_{i_1}',q_{j_l}',\dots,q_{j_2}'$ along $\partial B(b,r)$. Let $\gamma_i=T[b,p_i]$, $\sigma_i=T[b,q_i]$. For any $x,y\in\partial B(b,r)$ denote also by $P[x,y]$ the counterclockwise arc in $\partial B(b,r)$ from $x$ to $y$. 
Write $\overline{B}(b,r)=S_1\cup S_2\cup S_3$ where 
\begin{align*}
        \partial S_1&=P[p_{i_1}',p'_{i_2}]\cup  \gamma_{i_2} \cup \gamma_{i_1} \\
        \partial S_2&= P[p'_{i_2},p'_{i_l}]\cup  \gamma_{i_l} \cup \gamma_{i_2} \\
        \partial S_3&=P[p'_{i_l},p'_{i_1}]\cup  \gamma_{i_1} \cup \gamma_{i_l}.
    \end{align*}

Similar arguments with Case 1 show that $\sigma_{i_1}\subset S_1\cup S_3$, $\sigma_{i_l}\subset S_2\cup S_3$, and $\sigma_{i_2}\subset S_1\cup S_2$. Suppose that $q_{i_1}'\in \partial S_3$; if $q_{i_1}'\in \partial S_1$ similar arguments work. Since $q_{i_2}'\in P[q_{i_l}',q_{i_1}']$ it follows that either $\sigma_{i_l}\cup \sigma_{i_2}\subset S_2$ or that $\sigma_{i_l}\subset S_3$ and $\sigma_{i_2}\subset S_1$. We show the former and the latter follows with similar arguments. Note that $T[b,p_{i_l}]\cap T[b,q_{i_l}]= T[b,b']$ where $b'\in B_{i_l}$ is a branch point from Lemma \ref{lem: metric trees properties of BM}(ii). Let $S_2= R_1\cup R_2$ where 
    \begin{align*}
        &\partial R_1=P[p_{i_2}',q_{i_l}']\cup T[q_{i_l}',b]\cup T[b,p_{i_2}'] \\ &
        \partial R_2=P[q_{i_l}',p_{i_l}']\cup T[p_{i_l}',b']\cup T[b',q_{i_l}']. 
    \end{align*}
    Since $q_{i_2}'\in P[q_{i_l}',q_{i_1}']$, $T(q_{i_2}',b]\subset B(b,r)$ and $T$ is a tree it follows that $T[q_{i_2}',b]\subset R_2$ which is contradiction.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
\label{defn:BOP}
A homeomorphism $f:T\to T'$ between two planar trees is \textit{branch-orientation-preserving} if it satisfies the following property. Let $b\in T$ be a branch point, let $B_1,\dots,B_k$ be the branches of $T$ at $p$, and let $D,D'$ be Jordan domains, so that
\begin{enumerate}
\item $b\in D$ and $f(b)\in D'$,
\item for each $i\in \{1,\dots,k\}$ there exist $x_i \in \partial D\cap B_i$ and $y_i \in \partial D'\cap f(B_i)$ with $T[b,x_i)\subset D$, and $T'[f(b),y_i)\subset D'$.
\end{enumerate}
If $x_{i_1},\dots, x_{i_{k}}$ is a counterclockwise enumeration along $\partial D$, then $y_{i_1},\dots, y_{i_k}$ is a counterclockwise enumeration along $\partial D'$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
\label{def:tile decomposition}
Let $(T,d)$ be a metric tree. We say that $T$ admits a $C$-\emph{tile decomposition}
$\{\mathbf{X}^n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$ if there exists an increasing sequence of finite sets
$\emptyset=\mathbf{V}^0\subset \mathbf{V}^1\subset \mathbf{V}^2\subset\cdots,$
each consisting of double points of $T$, and a constant $C>17$, such that for every $n\in\mathbb{N}_0$, the collection $$\mathbf{X}^n=\left\{\overline{U}: U \text{ is a connected component of } T\setminus\mathbf{V}^n\right\}$$
is called the collection of \emph{$n$-tiles}, and the following properties hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[(i)] 
    \item Each $n$-tile contains at least three $(n+1)$-tiles and at most $C$ $(n+1)$-tiles.
    \item If $X,Y\in{\bf X}^n$ with $X\cap Y\neq \emptyset$, then $\frac{1}{C}\operatorname{diam} Y\leq\operatorname{diam} X\leq C\operatorname{diam} Y$. 
    \item If $X,Y\in{\bf X}^n$ with $X\cap Y=\emptyset$, then $\operatorname{dist}(X,Y)\geq\frac{1}{C}\operatorname{diam} Y$. 
    \item If $X\in{\bf X}^n$, $Y\in{\bf X}^{n+1}$ with $Y\subset X$, then $16\operatorname{diam} Y\leq\operatorname{diam} X\leq C\operatorname{diam} Y$. 
    \item For each $k\geq C$ we have
$\operatorname{diam}(Y)\leq \frac12\operatorname{diam}(X)$ for all $X\in {\bf X}^n$ and $Y \in {\bf X}^{n+k}$ with $X\cap Y\neq\emptyset$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- prop-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:tile-decomposition}
Every quasiconformal tree admits a $C$-tile decomposition with $C$ depending on the bounded turning constant. Moreover, if $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is its $C$-tile decomposition of $(T,d)$ and $f:(T,d)\to (T',d')$ is $\eta$-quasisymmetric, then the set
$$\{\mathbb{X}^n\}:=\{f(X):X\in{\bf X}^n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$$
is a $C'$-tile decomposition of $(T',d')$, where $C'$ depends only on $C$, the bounded turning constant of $T$, and $\eta$.
<!-- end prop-1 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Let $(T,d)$ be a quasiconformal tree and let $C_T$ be the bounded turning constant. Consider a new metric $\texttt{dd}$ on $T$ by $\texttt{dd}(x,y)=\operatorname{diam}_d T[x,y]$. By \cite[Lemma 2.5(iv)]{BM20} $(T,d)$ is $C_{T}$-bi-Lipschitz equivalent to $(T,\texttt{dd})$. Hence, the space $(T,\texttt{dd})$ is doubling and $1$-bounded turning. By \cite[Section 5]{BM20} there exists a sequence of finite sets ${\bf V}^1\subset {\bf V}^2\subset\dots$ consisting of double points of $(T,\texttt{dd})$ that are $\delta_1^n$-separated for some constant $\delta_1>0$ that depends on the doubling constant of $(T,\texttt{dd})$. Hence, for $\delta_0<\delta_1/C_{\bf T}^2$ the points in ${\bf V}^n$ are $\delta^n/C_T$-separated in $\mathbb{R}^2$ for every $\delta<\delta_0$. The above facts and \cite[page 275 (5.3),(5.4)]{BM20} give that for $X,Y\in{\bf X}^n$ disjoint $\delta^n/C_T\le\operatorname{diam} X\le3\beta\delta^n$ for some constant $\beta$ that depends only on $C_T$ and that $\operatorname{dist}_d (X,Y)\geq \delta^n/C_T$. After rescaling we may assume that $C_T>17$.
Assume further that 
\begin{align*}\label{eq: choice of epsilon}
    \delta < \min \{ \delta_0, (16\beta C_{{\bf T}}^2)^{-1}\}.
\end{align*}
Set $C=3\beta C_T$. It easy to verify now that $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ satisfies properties (ii)-(v) of Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}. Last, Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}(i) follows from \cite[Lemma 5.4(i)]{BM20} and \cite[Lemma 5.7(ii)]{BM20}. It follows that $(T,d)$ has $C$-tile decomposition $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ with $C$ depending only on $C_T$. In particular, the $C$-tile decomposition $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ satisfies the properties of \cite[Lemma 2.4]{BM22} and is therefore a \textit{quasi-visual approximation of $T$} according to Definition \cite[Definition 2.1]{BM22}. 

Let now $f:(T,d)\to (T',d')$ be an $\eta$-quasisymmetric map and let $({\mathbb X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a $C$-tile decomposition of $(T,d)$. By Proposition \cite[Proposition 2.7]{BM22}, $({\mathbb X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is a quasi-visual approximation of $T'$. Therefore, by \cite[Definition 2.1, Corollary 2.3]{BM22} and the fact that $f$ is a homeomorphism, it follows that $({\mathbb X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ satisfies the properties of Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition} for some constant $C'\geq 1$ that depends on $C_T$ and $\eta$. We do a small adjustment to show that $C'>17$. 

Note that if $X\in {\bf X}^n$, $Y\in{\bf X}^{n+1}$, then 
$$\frac{\operatorname{diam} Y}{\operatorname{diam} X}\leq 3\beta C_T\delta.$$
By \cite[Proposition 10.8]{Heinonen}, 
$$\frac{\operatorname{diam} f(Y)}{\operatorname{diam} f(X)}\leq \eta (6\beta C_T\delta)$$
By continuity of $\eta$ there exists $d>0$ such that $\eta(6\beta C_T\delta)<1/16$ for all $\delta< \frac{d}{6\beta C_T}$. Hence, taking $\delta< \frac{d}{6\beta C_T}$ and setting $C'=1/\eta(6\beta C_T\delta)$, we have that $({\mathbb X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ is a $C'$-tile decomposition with $C'$ depending only on $C_T$ and $\eta$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lem-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:properties of BM}
 \begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item If $U$ is a connected component of $T\setminus{\bf V}^n$, then $\overline{U}$ is a subtree of $T$ and $\emptyset\neq\partial U\subset {\bf V}^n$.
\item Two distinct $n$-tiles $X,Y$ have at most one point in common. Such a common point is in ${\bf V}^n$ and a boundary point of both $X$ and $Y$.
\item Each $v\in{\bf V}^n$ is contained in exactly two $n$-tiles. 
\item If $X$ is an $n$-tile with $x,y\in\partial X$ distinct, then $T(x,y)\subset X\setminus\partial X$ and it intersects at least three $(n+1)$-tiles contained in $X$.
\item  If $X$ is an $n$-tile with $x\in \partial X$, then there exists a unique $(n+1)$-tile $Y$ such that $x\in Y\subset X$. Also, each $(n+1)$-tile $Y$ is contained in a unique $n$-tile $X$.
\item  If $X$ is an $n$-tile and $v\in{\bf V}^n$, then $X$ is contained in the closure of one of the two components of $T \setminus \{v\}$ and disjoint from the other component.
\item If $X$ is an $n$-tile with $x,y\in \partial X$ distinct, then $|x-y|\geq\frac{\operatorname{diam} X}{C^2}$.
\item We have $\max\{\operatorname{diam} (X):X\in{\bf X}^n\}\to 0$ as $n\to\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-8 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
Claim (i) follows from \cite[Lemma 2.3(ii)]{BM20} and \cite[Lemma 5.1(i),(iii)]{BM20}. Claims (ii),(iii) follow from \cite[Lemma 5.1(iv),(v)]{BM20}. Claim (iv) follows from \cite[Lemma 5.4(ii), Lemma 5.5]{BM20}. Claim (v) follows from \cite[Lemma 5.1(vii),(ix)]{BM20}. Last, Claim (vi) follows from \cite[Lemma 5.1(ii)]{BM20}. 

We show (vii). By (v), $x\in X_1,y\in X_2$, where $X_1,X_2\subset X$ are $(n+1)$-tiles and by (ii),(iv) it follows that $X_1\cap X_2=\emptyset$. By Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}(iii),(iv) we have
   $$|x-y|\geq\operatorname{dist} (X_1,X_2)\geq \frac{\operatorname{diam} X_1}{C}\geq \frac{\operatorname{diam} X}{C^2}.$$

Finally, (viii) follows from Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}(v) and \cite[Corollary 2.3]{BM22}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lem-9: body_md -->
\label{lem: geometry of rhombuses}
For each $w\in A_{2m-2}^*$ and distinct $i,j\in A_{2m-2}$ we have that $R_{wi}\subset R_w$ and that $R_{wi}\cap R_{wj}=\{\psi_w(0)\}$. Moreover, there exists a constant $c>0$ that depends only on $m$ such that for each $x\in R_{wi}$ and $y\in R_{wj}$ distinct, we have
$$|x-y|\geq c(|x-\psi_w(0)|+|y-\psi_w(0)|).$$
<!-- end lem-9 -->
<!-- lem-10: body_md -->
\label{lem: properties of R_m}
For all $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and $w\in A_{2m-2}^n$, define the set 
$$R_w^*= R_w \cap \bigcup_{u\in A_{2m-2}^n\setminus\{w\}} R_u.$$
Then $\operatorname{card}(R_w^*)\in\{1,2\}$ for each $w\in A_{2m-2}^*\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$. In particular, if $w=w'j$ for some $j\in A_{2m-2}\setminus \{1,m\}$, then $\operatorname{card}(R_w^*)=1$. Moreover, if $R_u\cap R_v\neq\emptyset$ for some $u,v\in A_{2m-2}^n$ distinct, then there exist $i,j\in\{1,m\}$, and $w'\in A_{2m-2}^*$ such that $R_u\cap R_v=R_{ui}\cap R_{vj}=\{\psi_{w'}(0)\}$.
<!-- end lem-10 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
That $\operatorname{card}(R_w^*)\in\{1,2\}$ for any $w\in A_{2m-2}^*\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$, and that $\operatorname{card}(R_{w'j})=1$ for $j\in A_{2m-2} \setminus \{1,m\}$ follow as in \cite[Lemma 5.2(i)]{BM22}.

Fix now distinct $u,v\in A_{2m-2}^n$ and assume that $R_u\cap R_v\neq\emptyset$. Let $w\in A_{2m-2}^*$ be the longest word such that $u=wi_1\cdots i_{n-|w|}$ and $v=wj_1\cdots j_{n-|w|}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses}, $R_{wi_1}\cap R_{wj_1}=\{\psi_w(0)\}$, $R_u\subset R_{wi_1}$, and $R_v\subset R_{wj_1}$. Hence,     
$$\emptyset \neq \psi_w^{-1}(R_u\cap R_v) = R_{i_1\dots i_{n-|w|}}\cap R_{j_1\dots j_{n-|w|}}\subset \psi_w^{-1}(\{\psi_{w}(0)\})=\{0\}.$$

If $|w|=n-1$, then $u=wi_1$, and $v=wj_1$. The above equation and the fact that $\psi_m(\frac{1}{2})=-\frac{1}{2}$, $\psi_{i_1}(-\frac{1}{2})=\psi_{j_1}(-\frac{1}{2})=0$ shows that $R_{i_1m}\cap R_{j_1m}=\{0\}.$ Thus, 
$$R_{wi_1m}\cap R_{wj_1m}=\{\psi_w(0)\}.$$
    
If $|w|<n-1$, then we claim that $i_1\cdots i_{n-|w|}=i_1m1^{(n-|w|-2)}$ and that $j_1\cdots j_{n-|w|}=j_1m1^{(n-|w|-2)}$. First, note that $0\in R_{i_1m1^{(n-|w|-2)}}\cap R_{j_1m1^{(n-|w|-2)}}$ since $\psi_{i_1}(-\frac{1}{2})=\psi_{j_1}(-\frac{1}{2})=0$, $\psi_{1}(\frac{1}{2})=\frac{1}{2}$, and $\psi_{m}(\frac{1}{2})=-\frac{1}{2}$. Moreover, a simple geometric observation shows that $\frac{1}{2}\in R_i$ exactly when $i=1$, and that $-\frac{1}{2}\in R_j$ exactly when $j=m$. Hence, $0\notin R_{i_1k}\cup R_{j_1k}$, for $k\in A_{2m-1}\setminus\{m\}$, and $0\notin R_{i_1m1^{(l)}k'}\cup R_{j_1m1^{(l)}k'}$ for any integer $l\geq 0$, and $k'\in A_{2m-2}\setminus\{1\}$. This completes the proof of the claim.

Setting now $i=j=1$, we get 
    \[ R_{ui}\cap R_{vj}=R_{wi_1m1^{(n-|w|+1)}}\cap R_{wj_1m1^{(n-|w|+1)}}=\{\psi_w(0)\}. \qedhere \]
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{def: choice function}
An \emph{$m$-dictionary} is a set $\mathcal{W}\subset A_{2m-2}^*$, so that the following properties are satisfied.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(D1)] We have that $\{1, m\} \subset \mathcal{W}$. 
\item[(D2)] If $w\in \mathcal{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$, then $w^{\uparrow} \in \mathcal{W}$. 
\item[(D3)]  If $w \in \mathcal{W}$, then $\operatorname{card}{\{j\in A_{2m-2}:wj\in \mathcal{W}\}} \leq m$.
\item[(D4)] If $v,u\in \mathcal{W}$ are distinct with $|v|=|u|$ and $R_{vi}\cap R_{uj}\neq\emptyset$ for some $i,j\in \{1,m\}$, then $vi,uj \in\mathcal{W}$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- lem-11: body_md -->
\label{lem: m-dictionary non-empty}
Given an $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$ and an integer $n\ge 0$ define 
\[\mathcal{W}_n = \{ w\in \mathcal{W} : |w|=n\} \quad\text{and}\quad \mathcal{W}_{\infty} :=\{w\in A_{2m-2}^{\mathbb{N}}: w\{n\}\in\mathcal{W}_n \text{ for all }n\in\mathbb{N}\}. \]
For all integers $n\geq 0$, and $i\in \mathcal{W}_1$, $im1^{(n)}\in \mathcal{W}_{n+2}$, and $\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{im1^{(n)}}=\{0\}$. In particular, $\mathcal{W}_n\neq\emptyset$ and $\mathcal{W}_{\infty}\neq\emptyset$.
<!-- end lem-11 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
By (D1),(D2) we have $\varepsilon\in \mathcal{W}_0, \{1,m\}\subset\mathcal{W}_1$, and hence $\mathcal{W}_0,\mathcal{W}_1\neq\emptyset$. Moreover, note that for all integers $n\geq 0$, and distinct $i,j\in \mathcal{W}_1$, $0\in R_{im1^{(n)}}\cap R_{jm1^{(n)}}$ since $\psi_1(\frac{1}{2})=\frac{1}{2}$, $\psi_m(\frac{1}{2})=-\frac{1}{2}$, and $\psi_i(-\frac{1}{2})=\psi_j(-\frac{1}{2})=0$. Hence, by (D4), for all integers $n\geq 0$, $im1^{(n)}\in \mathcal{W}_{n+2}$. We conclude that $im1^{(\infty)}\in \mathcal{W}_{\infty}$. Last, since the sets $R_{im1^{(n)}}$ are compact, nested, and $\operatorname{diam} R_{im1^{(n)}}\to 0$, we have that $\{0\}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{im1^{(n)}}$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lem-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:consistency of eta}
Let $\mathcal{W}$ be an $m$-dictionary. For all $w\in \mathcal{W}$ there exists $j\in\{1,m\}$ such that $wj \in \mathcal{W}$.
<!-- end lem-12 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
We first show that for each $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and each $w\in\mathcal{W}_n$ there exists $u\in\mathcal{W}_n\setminus\{w\}$ such that $R_{w}\cap R_{u}\neq\emptyset$. The proof is by induction on $n$. Suppose first that $w\in\mathcal{W}_1$. By (D1), there exists $i\in (\{1,m\} \setminus \{w\})\cap\mathcal{W}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses}, $R_{w}\cap R_{i} \neq \emptyset$. Suppose now that the claim is true for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$. Fix $w\in \mathcal{W}_{n+1}$. By the induction hypothesis, there exists $u \in \mathcal{W}_n\setminus \{w^{\uparrow}\}$ such that $R_{w^{\uparrow}}\cap R_{u}\neq\emptyset$. By Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} there exist $i,j\in \{1,m\}$ such that $R_{w^{\uparrow}i}\cap R_{uj}\neq\emptyset$. Therefore, by (D4) we have $w^{\uparrow}i,uj\in \mathcal{W}$. If $w=w^{\uparrow}i$, then the induction is complete. If $w=w^{\uparrow}k$ for some $k\in A_{2m-2}\setminus\{i\}$, then by Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses} we have $R_{w^{\uparrow}i}\cap R_{w}\neq\emptyset$ and the proof of the claim is complete.

For the proof of the lemma, fix $w\in\mathcal{W}$.  If $w=\varepsilon$, then by (D1) we have $\{1,m\}\subset \mathcal{W}$. If $w\neq\varepsilon$, then by the claim there exists $u\in\mathcal{W}\setminus\{w\}$ such that $|w|=|u|$ and $R_{u}\cap R_{w}\neq\emptyset$. By Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} there exist $i,j\in\{1,m\}$ such that $R_{wj}\cap R_{ui}\neq\emptyset$. By (D4) we conclude that $wj\in \mathcal{W}$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lem-13: body_md -->
\label{lem: Tm,eta connected}
For every $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$, the set $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is non-empty, compact and connected.
<!-- end lem-13 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Fix an $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$. For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, let 
\[ J_{n}=\bigcup_{w\in \mathcal{W}_n}R_{w}.\]
    
By Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses}, if $w,wi\in \mathcal{W}$, then $R_{wi}\subset R_{w}$. Hence, by (D2),
$$J_{n+1} = \bigcup_{w\in \mathcal{W}_{n+1}}R_{w}\subset \bigcup_{w\in\mathcal{W}_n}R_{w}=J_{n}.$$
Since $\mathcal{W}_n\neq\emptyset$ for each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, the set $J_{n}$ is nonempty and compact as a finite union of compact nonempty sets. Therefore, $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is compact and by the nestedness of the sets $J_n$ it follows that $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is non-empty. 

We show now by induction on $n$ that each $J_{n}$ is connected. Assuming connectedness, by compactness and nestedness of the sets $J_{n}$, it follows that $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is connected. For $n=1$, by Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses}, $J_{1}$ is the union of a finite number of rhombuses $R_i$ that all intersect at the same point, so $J_{1}$ is connected. 

Assume now that $J_{n}$ is connected for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and let $u\in\mathcal{W}_n$. By Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} and the induction hypothesis,
$$\operatorname{card} (\{v\in\mathcal{W}_n\setminus\{u\}: R_{u}\cap R_{v}\neq\emptyset\})\in\{1,2\}.$$
Moreover, by Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses}, if $ui,ui'\in\mathcal{W}_{n+1}$, then $R_{ui}\cap R_{ui'}\neq\emptyset$. Therefore, since $J_{n+1}\subset J_{n}$ and $J_{n}$ is connected, it is enough to show that there exists $ui'\in\mathcal{W}_{n+1}$ such that $R_{ui'}\cap R_{u}^*\neq\emptyset$, where $R_{u}^*$ is as in Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m}. Fix $v\in\mathcal{W}_n$ distinct from $u$ such that $R_{v}\cap R_{u}\neq\emptyset$. By Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} there exist $j,j'\in \{1,m\}$ such that 
$$R_{u}\cap R_{v}=R_{uj}\cap R_{vj'}\neq\emptyset.$$ 
By (D4) it follows that $uj,vj'\in\mathcal{W}_{n+1}$ and we conclude that $R_{uj}\cap R_{u}^*\neq\emptyset$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lem-14: body_md -->
\label{lem: pi functions}
Let $\mathcal{W}$ be an $m$-dictionary. The ``projection'' map
\begin{equation}\label{eq:proj} 
\pi_{\mathcal{W}} : \mathcal{W}_{\infty} \to \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}},\quad \text{by}\quad 
\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{w\{n\}} = \{\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\} \quad \text{for each $w\in \mathcal{W}_{\infty}$}
\end{equation}
is a well-defined surjection. Moreover, $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(v)=\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)$ for distinct $v,w\in \mathcal{W}_{\infty}$ if and only if there exists $u\in\mathcal{W}$ and distinct $i,j \in A_{2m-2}$ such that $v=uim1^{(\infty)}$ and $w=ujm1^{(\infty)}$.
<!-- end lem-14 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
To show that $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}$ is well-defined, fix $w\in \mathcal{W}_{\infty}$. Since $w\{n\}\in \mathcal{W}_n$ for each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, the sets $R_{w\{n\}}$ are compact and nested and $\operatorname{diam} R_{w\{n\}}\to 0$. Hence, $\operatorname{card}(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}} R_{w\{n\}})=1$, so $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}$ is well-defined. 

Before proving the surjectivity of $\pi_\mathcal{W}$, we show that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pi-1(0)}
\pi_{\mathcal{W}}^{-1}(0)=\{im1^{(\infty)} : i\in \mathcal{W}_1\}.
\end{equation}
By Lemma \ref{lem: m-dictionary non-empty} $0\in \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ and $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(im1^{(\infty)})=0$.  Suppose now that $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)=0$ for some $w=i_1i_2\cdots\in\mathcal{W}_{\infty}$ where $i_j\in A_{2m-2}$. Then, $\psi_{i_1}(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{i_2\cdots i_n}) = 0$, which gives that $\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{i_2\cdots i_n} = -1/2$. A simple geometric observation shows now that $i_2=m$. Hence, $\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{i_3\cdots i_n} = 1/2$. Notice that $1/2\in R_i$ exactly when $i=1$, and that $1/2$ is a fix point for $\psi_1$. Thus, $i_3=i_4=\cdots=1$. Therefore, $w=i_1m1^{(\infty)}$ which shows \eqref{eq:pi-1(0)}.

To show surjectivity, fix $y\in \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$. For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, there exists $w_n \in \mathcal{W}_n$ such that $y\in R_{w_n}$. If for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $R_{w_{n+1}}\subset R_{w_n}$, then there exists $i_{n+1} \in A_{2m-2}$ such that $w_{n+1} = w_ni_{n+1}$. Setting $w = w_1i_2i_3\cdots$, we have that $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)=y$. Suppose now that $n\in\mathbb{N}$ is maximal such that $R_{w_{n+1}}\not\subset R_{w_n}$. By (D2) we may assume $w_{n+1}=ui$ for some $u\in\mathcal{W}_{n}$ and $i\in A_{2m-2}$. Let $v\in \mathcal{W}$ be the longest word such that $w_n=vi_1\cdots i_{n-|v|}$ and $u=vj_1\cdots j_{n-|v|}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses}, $R_{vi_1}\cap R_{vj_1}=\{\psi_{v}(0)\}$, $R_{w_n}\subset R_{vi_1}$, and $R_{u}\subset R_{vj_1}$. Hence, 
$$y\in R_{w_n}\cap R_{w_{n+1}}\subset  R_{w_n}\cap R_{u}=R_{vi_1\dots i_{n-|v|}}\cap R_{vj_1\dots j_{n-|v|}}\subset\{\psi_{v}(0)\}.$$
We conclude that $y=\psi_{v}(0)$. Thus, by \eqref{eq:pi-1(0)}, $y=\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(v1m1^{(\infty)})$.

To prove the second claim, suppose that $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(v)=\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)$ for some distinct $v,w\in\mathcal{W}_{\infty}$. Let $u\in\mathcal{\mathcal{W}}_n$ be the longest word such that $v=ui_1i_2\cdots$ and $w=uj_1j_2\cdots$ with $i_1,i_2,\dots\in A_{2m-2}$ and $j_1,j_2,\dots \in A_{2m-2}$. Then, 
$$\{\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(v)\}=\psi_{u}(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{i_1\cdots i_n})=\psi_{u}(\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{j_1\cdots j_n})=\{\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\}.$$
By maximality of $u$, $j_1\neq i_1$. Moreover, $\psi_{u}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(v))$ and $\psi_{u}^{-1}(\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w))$ are both in $R_{i_1}\cap R_{j_1}$. Therefore, by \eqref{eq:pi-1(0)} and Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses} the words $i_1i_2\cdots$ and $j_1j_2\cdots$ are in 
$$\{im1^{(\infty)}: i\in A_{2m-2}\}.$$
The reverse implication follows easily by \eqref{eq:pi-1(0)}.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
\label{def:dictfun}
Given an $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$, a function $\eta: \mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\infty} \to A_{2m-2}^*\cup A_{2m-2}^{\mathbb{N}}$ is a \emph{dictionary function} if it is injective and satisfies the following rules.
\begin{enumerate}
\item We have that {$\eta(\mathcal{W})\subset A_{2m-2}^*$} and $\eta(\mathcal{W}_{\infty}) \subset A_{2m-2}^{\mathbb{N}}$.
\item We have that $\eta(\varepsilon) = \varepsilon$.
\item For every $w\in\mathcal W$, there exists a permutation $\sigma_w$ of $A_{2m-2}$ such that $\sigma_w(i)=i$ for all $i\in F_w$, and $\eta(wi)=\eta(w)\sigma_w(i)$ for all $wi\in\mathcal W$. 
\item If $w\in \mathcal{W}_{\infty}$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$, then $\eta(w)\{n\}  = \eta(w\{n\})$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- lem-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:dictfun2}
Let $\eta: \mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\infty} \to A_{2m-2}^*\cup A_{2m-2}^{\mathbb{N}}$ be a dictionary function.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item For each $w\in \mathcal{W}$, $|\eta(w)|=|w|$. 
\item The image $\eta(\mathcal{W})$ is an $m$-dictionary.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-15 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:dictfun}
The first claim follows easily from (2) and (3) of the definition.

We show now that $\eta(\mathcal{W})$ is an $m$-dictionary. Property (D1) follows from (2) and (3) of the definition along with (D1) for $\mathcal{W}$. For (D2) we simply observe that by (3) of the definition and the injectivity of $\eta$, if $w\in \mathcal{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$, then $\eta(w)^{\uparrow} = \eta(w^{\uparrow})$. For property (D3), we observe that, by injectivity of $\eta$, we have $\eta(w)j =\eta(u)$ if and only if $u=wi$ for some $i$. Therefore, since $\mathcal{W}$ satisfies (D3),
\[\operatorname{card}\{j\in A_{2m-2} : \eta(w)j \in \eta(\mathcal{W})\} = \operatorname{card}\{i\in A_{2m-2} : wi \in \mathcal{W}\} \leq m.\]

To show (D4), we first show that if $R_{\eta(u)}\cap R_{\eta(w)}\neq\emptyset$ for distinct $u,w\in\mathcal W_n$, then $R_u\cap R_w\neq\emptyset$. Suppose that $w,u\in\mathcal{W}_n$, and let $v\in\mathcal W$ be their longest common initial word. Write $w=vi_1\cdots i_{n-|v|}$ and $u=vj_1\cdots j_{n-|v|}$ with $i_1\neq j_1$. By (3) and injectivity of $\eta$, the longest common initial word of $\eta(w)$ and $\eta(u)$ is $\eta(v)$. Set $d=n-|v|$. If $d=1$, then Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses} gives
\[R_w\cap R_u=R_{vi_1}\cap R_{vj_1}=\{\psi_v(0)\}.\]
Suppose now that $d\ge2$. Since $R_{\eta(w)}\cap R_{\eta(u)}\neq\emptyset$, following the proof of Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} we have $\eta(w)=\eta(v)i_1'm1^{(d-2)}$ and $\eta(u)=\eta(v)j_1'm1^{(d-2)}$ where $i_1',j_1'\in A_{2m-2}$. We claim that $i_2\cdots i_{d}=j_2\cdots j_{d}=m1^{(d-2)}$. Since $vi_1,vj_1\in\mathcal W$ are distinct, since $R_{vi_1}\cap R_{vj_1}=\{\psi_v(0)\}$, and since 
\[ \psi_v(0)=\psi_{vi_1}(-1/2)=\psi_{vj_1}(-1/2),\]
we have $m\in F_{vi_1}\cap F_{vj_1}$. By (3), we have $i_2=j_2=m$. If $d=2$, this proves the claim. Suppose that $d\geq3$ and note that $R_{vi_1m}\cap R_{vj_1m}=\{\psi_v(0)\}$ and
\[\psi_v(0)=\psi_{vi_1m}(1/2) =\psi_{vj_1m}(1/2).\]
Hence, $1\in F_{vi_1m}\cap F_{vj_1m}.$ Again, by (3), it follows that $i_3=j_3=1$. Continuing in similar fashion, we obtain $w=vi_1m1^{(d-2)}$ and $u=vj_1m1^{(d-2)}$. A simple inspection of the similarities now gives $R_w\cap R_u=\{\psi_v(0)\}$ which proves the claim.

Assume now that $R_{\eta(w)i}\cap R_{\eta(u)j}\neq\emptyset$ for some $i,j\in\{1,m\}$. Since 
\[ R_{\eta(w)i}\cap R_{\eta(u)j}\subset R_{\eta(w)}\cap R_{\eta(u)},\] 
the claim gives $R_w\cap R_u\neq\emptyset$. Let $v$ be the longest common initial word of $w,u$. The preceding argument shows that there exists $t\in\{-1/2,1/2\}$ such that
\[ \psi_w(t)=\psi_u(t)=\psi_v(0), \qquad \psi_{\eta(w)}(t)=\psi_{\eta(u)}(t) =\psi_{\eta(v)}(0).\]
Since $R_{\eta(w)i}\cap R_{\eta(u)j}\neq\emptyset$, it follows that $t\in R_i\cap R_j$. Applying $\psi_w$ and $\psi_u$, respectively, gives $\psi_v(0)\in R_{wi}\cap R_{uj}.$ Thus, $i\in F_w$ and $j\in F_u$. By (D4) for $\mathcal{W}$, $wi,uj\in\mathcal W$, and by (3), $\eta(wi)=\eta(w)i$, $\eta(uj)=\eta(u)j.$ Consequently, $\eta(w)i,\eta(u)j\in\eta(\mathcal W)$, which proves (D4).
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- lem-16: body_md -->
\label{lem: global homeo}
For any $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$, the space $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ $L$-bi-Lipschitz embeds into $\mathcal{T}^{A^*_{m}}$ with $L\geq 1$ depending only on $m$ and $\epsilon$. In particular, there exists a constant $C\geq 1$ depending only on $m$ such that $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is a $c_m$-quasiconvex tree with $C_m$-uniform branch separation, valence at most $m$, and Hausdorff dimension at most $1+\epsilon$. Here $C_m,c_m$ depend only on $m$.
<!-- end lem-16 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Fix an $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$. We construct a dictionary function $\eta$ such that $\eta(\mathcal{W}) \subset A_m^*$ and $\eta(\mathcal{W}_{\infty}) \subset A^{\mathbb{N}}_{2m-2}$. The construction of $\eta$ is in an inductive fashion. Set $\eta(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon$. Assume now that for some integer $n\geq 0$ we have defined an injective function
\[ \eta : \bigcup_{k=0}^n\mathcal{W}_k \to \bigcup_{k=0}^nA_{m}^k\]
so that property (2) holds. Fix $w\in \mathcal{W}_n$ and write 
\[ \{i_1,\dots,i_l\} = \{i\in A_{2m-2} : wi \in \mathcal{W}\}.\]
By (D3) we have $l\leq m$. If $i_r\in \{1,m\}$, then set $\eta(wi_r)=\eta(w)i_r$. Otherwise, assign $\eta(wi_r)=\eta(w)j_r$ with $j_r \in A_{m}\setminus \{1,m\}$ so that $j_1,\dots, j_l$ are distinct. 
                                                                                            
Proceeding inductively, we define $\eta$ on all of $\mathcal{W}$. Finally, define $\eta$ on $\mathcal{W}_{\infty}$ by $\eta(i_1i_2\cdots) = j_1j_2\cdots$ so that for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\eta(i_1\cdots i_n) = j_1\cdots j_n$. Applying an induction, it is easy to see that $\eta$ is a dictionary function. By properties (1),(2), and (3) of Definition \ref{def:dictfun}, it is easy to see that $\operatorname{diam}{R_w} = \operatorname{diam}{R_{\eta(w)}}$ for all $w\in\mathcal{W}$.

Let $\pi_{\mathcal{W}},\pi_{A^*_{m}}$ be as in Lemma \ref{lem: pi functions}. Define 
\[ g:\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}\to \mathcal{T}^{A^*_{m}}, \quad\text{by}\quad g\circ \pi_{\mathcal{W}} = \pi_{A^*_{m}}\circ (\eta| \mathcal{W}_{\infty}).\]
We first show that $g$ is a well-defined injection. Let $x\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ and let $w,v\in \mathcal{W}_{\infty}$ such that $w\neq v$ and $x=\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(w)=\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(v)$. By Lemma \ref{lem: pi functions} there exists $u\in\mathcal{W}$ such that $v,w\in\{uim1^{(\infty)}:i\in A_{2m-2}\}$ which, by design of $\eta$, gives 
\[\eta(v),\eta(w) \in \{\eta(ui)m1^{(\infty)}: i\in A_m\}\]
and it follows again from Lemma \ref{lem: pi functions} that $\pi_{A^*_{m}}(\eta(v))=\pi_{A^*_{m}}(\eta(w))$. Hence, $g$ is well-defined. Interchanging $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}$ and $\pi_{A^*_{m}}$, we get that $g^{-1}$ is well-defined so $g$ is injective.

We show now that $g$ is bi-Lipschitz. Let $x,y\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ distinct. Let $u,v\in\mathcal{W}_{\infty}$ such that $x=\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(u)$ and $y=\pi_{\mathcal{W}}(v)$. Since $x\neq y$, there exists $w\in\mathcal{W}$ such that $u=wi_1i_2\cdots$, $v=w j_1 j_2\cdots$, and $i_1\neq j_1$. Moreover, by Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses} there exists maximal $n\in\mathbb{N}$ such that $R_{wi_1\cdots i_n} \cap R_{wj_1\cdots j_n} \neq \emptyset$. We may assume that $n\geq 2$; the case where $n=1$ is similar. Following the proof of Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} we have $wi_1\cdots i_n = wi_1m1^{(n-2)}$ and $wj_1\cdots j_n = wj_1m1^{(n-2)}$ with either $i_{n+1} \neq 1$ or $j_{n+1} \neq 1$. Thus,
\[ |x-y| \simeq \operatorname{diam}{R_{wi_1m1^{(n-2)}}} \simeq (1/2)^{n}\operatorname{diam}{R_w}\]
with the implied constant depending only on $m$ and $\epsilon$.

By design of $\eta$, $\eta(u) = \eta(w)i_1'm1^{(n-2)}i_{n+1}'\cdots$ and $\eta(v) = \eta(w)j_1'm1^{(n-2)}j_{n+1}'\cdots$ with either $i_{n+1}' \neq 1$ or $j_{n+1}' \neq 1$. Therefore,
\[ |g(x)-g(y)| \simeq \operatorname{diam}{R_{\eta(wi_1m1^{(n-2)})}} \simeq (1/2)^{n}\operatorname{diam}{R_{\eta(w)}} = (1/2)^{n}\operatorname{diam}{R_{w}}\]
with the implied constant depending only on $m$ and $\epsilon$.

By Lemma \ref{lem: Tm,eta connected} $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is compact and connected. Arguments similar to those of \cite[Proposition 6.1, Corollary 10.2, Lemma 10.6]{CGIV} show that $\mathcal{T}^{A^*_{m}}$ is a quasiconvex tree with uniform branch separation and valence exactly $m$. Therefore, $g(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}})$ is a quasiconvex tree with uniform branch separation and valence at most $m$ as a subtree of $\mathcal{T}^{A^*_{m}}$. Since these properties are bi-Lipschitz invariant, $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is also a quasiconvex tree with uniform branch separation and valence at most $m$. Finally, the Hausdorff dimension of $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is at most that of $\mathcal{T}^{A^*_{m}}$ which by \eqref{eq:Hausdim} and by Hutchinson \cite{Hu81}, is at most $1+\epsilon$.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- lem-17: body_md -->
\label{lem:treearc}
If $n\in\mathbb{N}$, and $w,u\in\mathcal{W}_n$ are distinct with $R_u\cap R_w\neq\emptyset$, then $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}[x,y]\subset R_u\cup R_w$ for each $x\in \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}\cap R_w$ and $y\in \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}\cap R_u$.
<!-- end lem-17 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
We show the claim by induction on $n$. If $n=1$, then the claim follows from Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses} and the fact that $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is a tree. Suppose now that the claim is true for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and let $w,u\in \mathcal{W}_{n+1}$ with $R_w\cap R_u\neq\emptyset$, and let $x\in R_w$ and $y\in R_u$. Write $w=w'i$, $u=u'j$ where $w',u'\in \mathcal{W}_n$ and $i,j\in A_{2m-2}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses}, $\emptyset\neq R_{w}\cap R_u\subset R_{w'}\cap R_{u'}$. By the induction hypothesis, $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}[x,y]\subset R_{w'}\cup R_{u'}$. Since $x\in R_{w'i}$ and $y\in R_{u'j}$, by Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} and the fact that $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is a tree, we get $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}[x,y]\subset R_w\cup R_u$ and the proof of the claim is complete.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- prop-2: body_md -->
\label{prop: enumeration of T=enumeration of Tmeta}
Let $m\in\{2,3,\dots,\}$ and $\epsilon>0$.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item If $\mathcal{W}$ is an $m$-dictionary, then $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is a $c_m$-quasiconvex tree, with $C_m$-uniform branch separation, Hausdorff dimension at most $1+\epsilon$, and valence at most $m$. Here, $C_m,c_m$ depend only on $m$.
\item If $T\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ is a quasiconformal tree with uniform branch separation and valence at most $m$, then there exist an $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$ and a branch-orientation-preserving $\theta$-quasisymmetric homeomorphism $g:T\to\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ such that $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is  semi-geodesic. The distortion $\theta$ depends only on $m$, $\epsilon$, the bounded turning constant of $T$, and the uniform branch separation constant of $T$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-2 -->
<!-- cor-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:f(T) is Tmeta} 
Suppose that $(T,d)$ is a quasiconformal tree with uniform branch separation and valence at most $m$. Then there exists an $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$ and a quasisymmetric homeomorphism  $f:T \to \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$.
<!-- end cor-2 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By \cite[Theorem 1.3(i),(iii)]{CGIV}, there exists a quasisymmetric embedding $f: T \to \mathcal{T}^{A^*_{m}}$. Clearly $f(T)\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ is a quasiconformal tree with uniform branch separation and valence at most $m$ as a connected closed subset of $\mathcal{T}^{A_m^*}$. Thus, by Proposition \ref{prop: enumeration of T=enumeration of Tmeta}, there exists an $m$-dictionary $\mathcal{W}$ and a quasisymmetric map $g:f(T)\to \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$. Thus, the map $h=g\circ f:T\to\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ is a quasisymmetric homeomorphism.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lem-18: body_md -->
\label{lem:orientation of T2m-2}
                                                 
Let $w\in A_{2m-2}^*$ and let $D$ be a Jordan domain with $\psi_w(0)\in D$ and $\partial D\cap \mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_{wi}\neq\emptyset$ for all $i\in A_{2m-2}$. Then for each $i\in A_{2m-2}$ there exists $p_{wi}\in \mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_{wi}\cap\partial D$ such that $\mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_{wi}[\psi_w(0),p_{wi})\subset D$. The counterclockwise enumeration of the points $\{p_{wi}\}_{i\in A_{2m-2}}$ along $\partial D$, starting from $p_{w1}$, is $p_{w1},\dots, p_{w(2m-2)}.$
<!-- end lem-18 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
For each $i\in A_{2m-2}$ denote by $B_{i}$ the unique component of $\mathcal{T}^{2m-2}\setminus \{\psi_w(0)\}$ such that $\mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_{wi} \subset \overline{B_i}$. Then for each $i\in A_{2m-2}$, $\mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_{wi}$ is a subtree of $\overline{B_{i}}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: maximal arcs}(i) for each $i\in A_{2m-2}$ there exists $p_{wi}\in \mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_{wi}\cap\partial D$ such that $\mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_{wi}[\psi_w(0),p_{wi})\subset D$. It remains to find the counterclockwise enumeration starting from $p_{w1}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: maximal arcs}(ii), we may assume that $D=B(\psi_w(0),r)$ as the enumeration is independent of the Jordan domain. Moreover, since $D\cap \mathcal{T}^{2m-2} = D\cap \mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_w$, we may assume that $w=\varepsilon$. Recall that $\mathcal{T}^{2m-2}_i \subset R_i$ for each $i=1,\dots,2m-2$. The proof now follows from the simple geometric observation that rhombuses are counterclockwise enumerated as $R_1,R_2,\dots,R_{2m-2}$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lem-19: body_md -->
\label{lem:dictqs}
Let $\eta: \mathcal{W}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\infty} \to A_{2m-2}^*\cup A_{2m-2}^{\mathbb{N}}$ be a dictionary function. There exists a quasisymmetric homeomorphism $h:\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}\to \mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal{W})}$ such that $h(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}_w) = \mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal{W})}_{\eta(w)}$ for all $w\in \mathcal{W}$. The distortion of $h$ depends only on $m$ and $\epsilon$.
<!-- end lem-19 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Define $h:\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}\to \mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal{W})}$ by $$h\circ\pi_{\mathcal{W}}=\pi_{\eta(\mathcal{W})}\circ(\eta| \mathcal{W}_{\infty})$$
where $\pi_{\mathcal{W}}$ and $\pi_{\eta(\mathcal{W})}$ are as in Lemma \ref{lem: pi functions}. Since $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal W}$ and $\mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal W)}$ are connected doubling spaces, by \cite[Theorem 10.19]{Heinonen} it suffices to show that $h$ is $H$-weakly quasisymmetric with $H$ depending only on $m,\epsilon$. Consequently, $g:=h\circ f$ is a $\theta$-quasisymmetric map with $\theta$ depending on $m,\epsilon$, the bounded turning constant of $T$, and the uniform branch separation constant.

We first show that if $u,v\in\mathcal W$ with $R_u\cap R_v\neq\emptyset$ and sets $R_u,R_v$ are not subsets of each other, then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:comparability of intersecting rhombuses}
\frac{\operatorname{diam} R_u}{\operatorname{diam} R_v}\simeq_{m,\epsilon}\frac{\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(u)}}{\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(v)}}.
\end{equation}
Recall the contraction ratios $\lambda_i$ from \eqref{eq:psi} and set $r:=\min_{i\in \{1,\dots,m\}} \lambda_i$. Let $u,v\in\mathcal W$ such that $R_u\cap R_v\neq\emptyset$ and neither $u$ nor $v$ is initial subword of each other. Write $u=wi\alpha$ and $v=wj\beta$, where $i\neq j$ and $\alpha,\beta\in A_{2m-2}^*$, and write $\eta(wi)=\eta(w)i'$ and $\eta(wj)=\eta(w)j'$. By the geometry of the rhombuses, it follows that each of $\alpha,\beta$ is either $\varepsilon$ or of the form $m1^{(l)}$, for some $l\geq 0$. Consequently, by construction of $\eta$,
\[\frac{\operatorname{diam} R_u}{\operatorname{diam} R_v} =\frac{\lambda_i}{\lambda_j}\,2^{|\beta|-|\alpha|} \simeq_{m,\epsilon} \frac{\lambda_{i'}}{\lambda_{j'}}\,2^{|\beta|-|\alpha|} = \frac{\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(u)}}{\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(v)}}.\]

Fix now $x,y,z\in \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}$ such that $|x-y|\leq|x-z|$. We may assume that $x,y,z$ are distinct. Choose $u,v\in\mathcal W_\infty$ with $x=\pi_{\mathcal W}(u)$ and $z=\pi_{\mathcal W}(v)$. Write $u=wi_1i_2\cdots$ and $v=wj_1j_2\cdots$, where $i_1\neq j_1$. Let $n$ be maximal such that $R_{wi_1\cdots i_n}\cap R_{wj_1\cdots j_n}\neq\emptyset$, and set $u':=wi_1\cdots i_n$, $v':=wj_1\cdots j_n$. Write $R_{u'}\cap R_{v'}=\{b\}$, and by Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m} there exist $e,f\in\{1,m\}$ such that $b\in R_{u'e}\cap R_{v'f}$. By Lemma \ref{lem:treearc}, $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}[x,z]\subset R_{u'}\cup R_{v'}$. More precisely,
\begin{equation*}
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}[x,z] \subset R_{u'i_{n+1}}\cup R_{u'e}\cup R_{v'f} \cup R_{v'j_{n+1}}.
\end{equation*}
By maximality of $n$ either $i_{n+1}\neq e$ or $j_{n+1}\neq f$. Without loss of generality, assume the former and set $s=\operatorname{diam} R_{u'e}$. It follows that $\ell(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{W}}[x,z])\geq s$. In conjuction with quasiconvexity of $\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{{W}}}$ and the above equation we have $|x-z|\simeq_{m,\epsilon}s$. Moreover, by definition of $h$ and quasiconvexity of $\mathcal{T}^{{\eta (\mathcal{W})}}$
\begin{equation}\label{eq: quasiconvexity of T^eta(W)}
\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(u'e)} \leq \ell\bigl(\mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal W)}[h(x),h(z)]\bigr) \lesssim_{m} |h(x)-h(z)|.
\end{equation}

Consider now the connected set $\Gamma:=\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal W}[x,y]\cup \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal W}[x,z]$. By quasiconvexity and the assumption $|x-y|\leq|x-z|$, there is a constant $C$, depending only on $m$, such that $\Gamma\subset\overline{B}(x,C|x-z|).$ Define
\[ \mathcal M_s:= \left\{ v\in\mathcal W\setminus\{\varepsilon\}: \operatorname{diam} R_v\leq s < \operatorname{diam} R_{v^\uparrow}\text{ and }R_v\cap\Gamma\neq\emptyset \right\}.\]
The rhombuses $\{R_v\}_{v\in\mathcal{M}_s}$ cover $\Gamma$, have pairwise disjoint interiors, satisfy $rs<\operatorname{diam} R_v\leq s$, lie in $\overline{B}(x,C|x-z|+s)$, and satisfy $\operatorname{area}(R_v)\simeq_{m,\epsilon} s^2$. Hence,
\[\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{M}_s)\lesssim_{m,\epsilon}s^{-2}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{M}_s}\operatorname{area}(R_v) \lesssim s^{-2}(C|x-z|+s)^2\lesssim_{m,\epsilon} 1.\]

The sets $\{R_v\cap\Gamma\}_{v\in\mathcal{M}_s}$, form a finite closed cover of the connected set $\Gamma$. Their intersection is therefore connected. It follows from \eqref{eq:comparability of intersecting rhombuses} that there exists $L>0$ depending only on $m,\epsilon$ such that $\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(v)} \leq L^{\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{M}_s)}\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(u'e)}.$ By definition of $h$ we have that $h(\Gamma)\subset \bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{M}_s}R_{\eta(v)}.$ This union is connected, because intersections along $\Gamma$ are preserved by $h$. It follows that
\begin{align*} 
|h(x)-h(y)| \leq \operatorname{diam} \left(\bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{M}_s}R_{\eta(v)}\right) \leq \sum_{v\in\mathcal{M}_s}\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(v)}&\leq NL^{\operatorname{card}(\mathcal{M}_s)}\operatorname{diam} R_{\eta(u'e)}\\
&\lesssim_{m,\epsilon} |h(x)-h(z)|,
\end{align*}
where the last inequality follows from \eqref{eq: quasiconvexity of T^eta(W)}. Thus $h$ is $H$-quasisymmetric with $H$ depending only on $m,\epsilon$.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Fix $m\in \{2,3,\dots\}$ and $\epsilon>0$. Claim (i) follows from Lemma \ref{lem: global homeo}. 

To show Claim (ii), let $T$ as in the statement and let $f:T\to \mathcal{T}^{A_m^*}$ be a quasisymmetric embedding given from \cite[Theorem 1.3(i),(iii)]{CGIV}. The distortion of $f$ depends on the bounded turning constant of $T$, its uniform branch separation and $\epsilon$. Clearly, $T':=f(T)$ is a quasiconformal tree with uniform branch separation and valence at most $m$ as a connected closed subset of $\mathcal{T}^{A^*_m}$. 

There exists a longest word $w\in A_m^*$ such that $T' \subset \psi_w(\mathcal{T}^{A^*_m})$. Replacing $f$ by $f\circ \psi_w$, we may assume that there exist two distinct $i_1,i_2 \in A_m$ such that $\operatorname{diam}(T'\cap R_{i_1})>0$ and $\operatorname{diam}(T'\cap R_{i_2})>0$.
Let $h:\mathcal{T}^{A_m^*}\to \mathcal{T}^{A^*_m}$ be the bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism defined by
\begin{align*}
&h|\psi_{i_1}(\mathcal{T}^{A^*_m}) = \psi_1\circ\psi_{i_1}^{-1},\quad h|\psi_{1}(\mathcal{T}^{A^*_m}) = \psi_{i_1}\circ\psi_{1}^{-1},\\
&h|\psi_{i_2}(\mathcal{T}^{A^*_m}) = \psi_m\circ\psi_{i_2}^{-1},\quad h|\psi_{m}(\mathcal{T}^{A^*_m}) = \psi_{i_2}\circ\psi_{m}^{-1}\\
&h|\psi_i(\mathcal{T}^{A^*_m}) = \text{Id}\quad\text{for $i\not\in\{1,m,i_1,i_2\}$}.
\end{align*}
Composing $f$ with $h$, we may assume that $i_1=1$ and $i_2=m$.

Define now the set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:def of V}
    \mathcal{V}=\{w\in A_m^*: \operatorname{diam}(T'\cap R_w)>0\}\subset A^*_m.
\end{equation}
We show that $\mathcal{V}$ is an $m$-dictionary. 

By design of $f$, $\operatorname{diam}(T'\cap R_{1})>0$ and $\operatorname{diam}(T'\cap R_{m})>0$. Also, if $\operatorname{diam}(T'\cap R_w)>0$, then clearly $\operatorname{diam}(T'\cap R_{w^{\uparrow}})>0$. Therefore, properties (D1) and (D2) hold. Property (D3) is clear since $\mathcal{V} \subset A^*_m$. 

We now show (D4). Suppose now that $n\in\mathbb{N}$, that $w,u\in\mathcal{W}_n$ are distinct, and let $i,j\in \{1,m\}$ such that $R_{wi}\cap R_{uj}\neq \emptyset$. Then there exist $x\in (R_w\cap T')\setminus R_w^*$ and $y\in (R_u\cap T')\setminus R_u^*$. Moreover, by Lemma \ref{lem: properties of R_m},
\[ \emptyset \neq R_{wi}\cap R_{uj} \subset R_w \cap R_u \subset R_w^*\cap R_u^* \]
with the latter set containing only a single point $z$. In conjunction with Lemma \ref{lem:treearc}, we have that $z\in T'[x,y]\subset R_w\cup R_u$. Since $z\notin\{x,y\}$ there exists a subarc of $T'[x,y]$ that lies in $R_{wi}\cup R_{uj}$. We conclude that $wi,uj\in \mathcal{V}$ which gives (D4). 

We show that $T' =\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{V}}$. Let $x,y\in T'$ distinct. Since $T'\subset \mathcal{T}^{A_m^*}$, \eqref{eq: T^m rhombus def} and the design of $f$ give $x\in(R_{wi}\cap T')\setminus R_{wi}^*$ or $y\in (R_{wj}\cap T')\setminus R_{wj}^*$ for some $i,j\in A_m$ distinct. By Lemma \ref{lem: geometry of rhombuses} and the claim above, we have $T'[x,y]\subset R_{wi}\cup R_{wj}$. Thus, $wi,wj\in \mathcal{V}$ and $x,y\in \mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{V}}$ which gives $T'\subset\mathcal{T}^\mathcal{V}$. For the reverse inclusion, let $y\in \mathcal{T}^\mathcal{V}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: pi functions}, $y=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}R_{w\{n\}}$ where $w\{n\}\in\mathcal{V}_n$ for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$. Let $z_n\in T'\cap R_{w\{n\}}$. It follows that $z_n\to y$ and since $T'$ is closed, $y\in T'$. Hence, $T'=\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{V}}$.

We now construct a dictionary function $\eta$ on $\mathcal{V}\cup\mathcal{V}_{\infty}$ in an inductive manner. Let $\eta(\varepsilon)=\varepsilon$. Assume now that for some integer $n\geq 0$ we have defined an injective function
\[ \eta : \bigcup_{k=0}^n\mathcal{V}_k \to \bigcup_{k=0}^nA_{m}^k\]
so that property (3) of Definition \ref{def:dictfun} holds. Fix $w\in \mathcal{V}_{n}$, let $x_w=f^{-1}(\psi_w(0))$ and let $\operatorname{Val}(x_w)=p$. By Lemma \ref{lem:consistency of eta}, one of $w1,wm$ is in $\mathcal{V}_{n+1}$. 

\emph{Case 1}. Suppose first that both $w1,wm\in\mathcal{V}_{n+1}$. Let $D$ be a Jordan domain such that $D\cap f^{-1}(R_{wi})\neq\emptyset$ for all $i\in A_m$ such that $wi\in \mathcal{V}$. Recalling that $T'=\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{V}}$ we have that $\bigcup_{i\in A_m}f^{-1}(R_{wi})$ is a tree. Hence, Lemma \ref{lem: maximal arcs}(i) asserts the existence of $y_{wi}\in f^{-1}(R_{wi})$ such that $f^{-1}(R_{wi})[x_w,y_{wi})\subset D$. Let now $y_{w{i_1}},\dots, y_{w{i_p}}$ be a counterclockwise enumeration along $\partial D$, starting from $y_{wi_1}$. By Lemma \ref{lem: maximal arcs}(ii) this enumeration is independent of the domain $D$. Without loss of generality, we may assume that $i_1=1$. Let $j\in \{1,\dots,p\}$ such that $i_{j}=m$. Let $i_{l_1}<\cdots<i_{l_{j}}$ and $i_{l_{j+1}}<\cdots<i_{l_p}$ be rearrangements of the indices $i_1,\dots,i_{j}$ and $i_{j+1},\dots,i_p$ in increasing order, respectively. For each $k\in \{1,\dots, j\}$ set $\eta(w{i_k})=\eta(w)i_{l_k}$ and for each $k\in \{j+1,\dots, p\}$ set $\eta(w{i_k})=\eta(w)({i_{l_k}}+m-2)$.

\emph{Case 2}. Suppose now that $w1\in\mathcal V_{n+1}$ but $wm\notin\mathcal V_{n+1}$. The construction is similar to Case 1, with the following changes. The counterclockwise enumeration starts with $i_1=1$, but choose $j$ so that $i_j=\max\{i:wi\in\mathcal V_{n+1}\}.$ We make the same assignments as above, except that we set $\eta(wi_j)=\eta(w)m.$

\emph{Case 3}. Suppose that $wm\in\mathcal V_{n+1}$ but $w1\notin\mathcal V_{n+1}$. The construction is similar to Case 1, except that the counterclockwise enumeration starts with $i_1=\min\{i:wi\in\mathcal V_{n+1}\}$. Choose $j$ so that $i_j=m$, and make the same assignments as above, except that $\eta(wi_1)=\eta(w)1.$

In both Case 2 and Case 3, these modifications fix every letter in $F_w$. Proceeding inductively, we define $\eta$ on all of $\mathcal{V}$. Finally, define $\eta$ on $\mathcal{V}_{\infty}$ by $\eta(i_1i_2\cdots) = j_1j_2\cdots$ so that for all $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $\eta(i_1\cdots i_n) = j_1\cdots j_n$. Applying an induction, it is easy to see that $\eta$ is a dictionary function. By Lemma \ref{lem:dictfun}(ii) $\eta(\mathcal{V})$ is an $m$-dictionary and by Lemma \ref{lem:dictqs}, there exists a quasisymmetric map $h:\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{V}} \to \mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal{V})}$ such that $h(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{V}}_w) = \mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal{V})}_{\eta(w)}$ for each $w\in \mathcal{V}$. Set 
\[ g= h\circ f : T \to \mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal{V})}.\]

For each $w\in \mathcal{V}$, let $T_w=g^{-1}(\mathcal{T}^{\eta(\mathcal{V})}_{\eta(w)})$. Let $y_{wi_1},\dots,y_{wi_{\operatorname{Val}(x_w)}}$ as in the statement. Without loss of generality, we may assume that $i_1=1$. Note that, by the construction of $\eta$, the words $\{\eta(wi)\}_{i\in A_{2m-2},\,wi\in\mathcal W}$ are enumerated according to increasing values of $i$, beginning with $\eta(w1)$. On the other hand, by Lemma \ref{lem:orientation of T2m-2}, the points $\{p_{wi}\}_{i\in A_{2m-2}}\subset \mathcal{T}^{2m-2}$ are also enumerated according to increasing values of $i$, beginning with $p_{w1}$. Hence, the corresponding points $\{z_{\eta(wi)}\}_{wi\in\mathcal W}$ inherit the same ordering: $z_{\eta(w1)},\dots, z_{\eta(wi_{\operatorname{Val}(x_w)})}$. We conclude that $g$ is branch-orientation-preserving map. 

Finally, let $\mathcal W=\eta(\mathcal V)$, and define
\[V_n:=\bigcup_{k=0}^n\bigcup_{u\in A_{2m-2}^k}\psi_u([-1/2,1/2])\quad\text{and}\quad V:=\bigcup_{n\geq1}V_n.\]
Note that each $V_n$ is a tree consisting of finitely many line segments and that $\mathcal{T}^{2m-2}=\overline{V}$. 

We observe first that $\mathcal T^{2m-2}\setminus \mathcal{L}(\mathcal T^{2m-2})\subset V$. To see this fix $p\in\mathcal T^{2m-2}\setminus \mathcal{L}(\mathcal T^{2m-2})$. Two distinct components of $\mathcal T^{2m-2}\setminus\{p\}$ are open in $\mathcal T^{2m-2}$ and therefore contain points of $V$. The arc joining these points lies in some $V_n$ and contains $p$. 

Since $\mathcal{T}^\mathcal{W}\subset \mathcal{T}^{2m-2}$, we conclude that $\mathcal T^{\mathcal W}\setminus \mathcal L(\mathcal T^{\mathcal W})\subset V$. Let now $b\in \mathcal T^{\mathcal W}\setminus \mathcal L(\mathcal T^{\mathcal W})$ and let $B_1,B_2$ be the biggest (in diameter) component of $\mathcal T^{\mathcal W} \setminus\{b\}$. Then for $i\in \{1,2\}$ there exist $x_i\in B_{i}\cap V$. By the uniqueness of arcs and Lemma \ref{lem: metric trees properties of BM}(i), $b\in \mathcal{T}^\mathcal{W}(x_1,x_2)=V_n(x_1,x_2)$ for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$. In particular $\mathcal{T}^\mathcal{W}[x_1,x_2]$ is a polygonal arc. If $b$ is not a corner of this arc, a sufficiently short subarc containing $b$ is a line segment with $b$ in its interior. 

Suppose now that $b$ is a corner which means that $b=\psi_u(0)$ for some $u\in \mathcal{W}$. By the construction of $\eta$, $u1,um\in\mathcal W$, and both $\mathcal T^{\mathcal W}\cap R_{u1}$ and $\mathcal T^{\mathcal W}\cap R_{um}$ are nondegenerate. Choose non-leaf points in these two sets different from $b$. The arcs joining them to $b$ are polygonal and lie in the respective rhombuses. Since $b=\psi_{ui}(-1/2)$ for $i\in\{1,m\}$, their initial edges from $b$ lie in $\psi_{ui}([-1/2,1/2])$. Moreover, 
$$\psi_{u1}([-1/2,1/2]) \cup \psi_{um}([-1/2,1/2]) = \psi_u([-1/2,1/2])$$
with the latter being a line segment. Thus, choosing $x,y$ sufficiently close to $b$ on these respective arcs, $\mathcal T^{\mathcal W}[x,y]$ is a line segment containing $b$ in its interior.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- prop-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:polygonal curves}
Let $T\subset \mathbb{R}^2$ be a $C_T$-bounded turning tree with $\operatorname{diam}{T}=1$ and let $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ be a $C$-tile decomposition. There exist
\begin{enumerate}
\item an integer $N\in\mathbb{N}$ depending only on $C$, and a nonempty set $\mathscr{W} \subset\{1,\dots,N\}^*$,
\item positive integers $(k_X)_{X\in{\bf X}^*}$ such that $0<k_Y-k_X\lesssim_{C,C_T} 1$ for any $X\in{\bf X}^n$, $Y\in{\bf X}^{n+1}$, with $Y\subset X$,
\item a collection $\{x_w : w\in\mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\}$ of double points of $T$ and a collection $\{\zeta_w:w\in\mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}\}$ of arcs,
\item a nested sequence of Jordan domains $\Omega_1\supset\Omega_2\supset\cdots\supset T$, 
\end{enumerate}
so that 
                               
if $\delta_X:=2^{-k_X}$ and $\mathscr{W}_n:=\{w\in \mathscr{W}:|w|=n\}$, then the next properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item (Properties of $\mathscr{W}$.)
\begin{enumerate}
                                                
\item For each $w\in \mathscr{W}$ there exists $N_w \in \{2,\dots,N\}$ such that $wi\in \mathscr{W}$ if and only if $i\in\{1,\dots,N_w\}$.
\item If $w\in\mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$, then $w^{\uparrow} \in \mathscr{W}$.
\end{enumerate}      
\item (Properties of points $x_w$.)
\begin{enumerate}
\item For all $w\in \mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$, $x_w=x_{w1^{(n)}}$.
\item For all $w\in \mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$ there exist unique distinct $X_w^1,X_w^2\in {\bf X}^{|w|}$ such that $\{x_w\}=X_w^1\cap X_w^2$.
\item For all $w\in \mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$ there exists unique $u\in\mathscr{W}_{|w|} \setminus\{w\}$ such that $x_w=x_u$.
\item If $v,u\in \mathscr{W}$ with $|v|=|u|$ and $x_v\neq x_u$,  
$$|x_v-x_u| \gtrsim_{C_T,C} \max\{\delta_{X_v^1},\delta_{X_v^2},\delta_{X_u^1},\delta_{X_u^2}\}.$$
\item For each $x\in T$ and $n\in\mathbb{N}$, there exists $w\in \mathscr{W}_n$, and $X_w\in{\bf X}^n$ such that $|x-x_w| \lesssim_{C_T,C} \delta_{X_w}$. 
\end{enumerate}

\item (Properties of arcs $\zeta_w$.)
\begin{enumerate}
\item For each $w\in \mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$, arc $x_w$ is an endpoint of $\zeta_w$ and $\zeta_w \subset \overline{\Omega_{|w|}}$.
\item If $w\in \mathscr{W}\setminus\{\varepsilon\}$ and $m\geq |w|$, then $\zeta_w$ intersects $\partial\Omega_m$ at exactly one point which we denote by $p_{w1^{(m-|w|)}}$.
\item If $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and $w \in \mathscr{W}_n$, then $\zeta_{w1} = \zeta_w \cap \overline{\Omega_{n+1}}$, $\operatorname{diam}(\zeta_w\setminus\zeta_{w1}) \simeq_{C_T,C} 2^{-t_w}$ and $\zeta_w\setminus\zeta_{w1}\subset \mathcal{G}_{2^{-t_w}}$, where $t_w\in\mathbb{N}$ is chosen such that
\[2^{-t_w}<(16C)^{-1}\min\{\delta_{X_{w1}^1},\delta_{X^2_{w1}}\}\leq 2^{-t_w+1}.\]
\item For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, the points $\{p_w:w\in\mathscr{W}_n\}$ are oriented on $\partial\Omega_n$ according to the cyclic lexicographic order of $\mathscr{W}_n$.
\item Let $w,u \in \mathscr{W}$ with $x_w\neq x_u$. If $v\in \mathscr{W}_t$ with $t=\max\{|w|+1,|u|+1\}$, then $$\operatorname{dist}(\zeta_w,\zeta_u)\gtrsim_{C_T,C} \max\{\delta_{X_{v}^1},\delta_{X_{v}^2},\delta_{X_{v}^1},\delta_{X_{v}^2}\}.$$
\end{enumerate}
\item (Properties of domains $\Omega_n$.) For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, let $\Gamma_n:=\partial\Omega_n$.
\begin{enumerate}
                                                                                                                                                                                                 
\item For each $z\in \Gamma_n$ there exists $X\in{\bf X}^n$ such that $\delta_X\lesssim_C\operatorname{dist}(z,T)< 8\delta_X$.
\item Let $n\in\mathbb{N}$ and $w,u\in \mathscr{W}_n$ such that $u$ is to the left of $w$. There exists unique $j\in \{1,2\}$ such that $x_w,x_u\in \partial X_w^j$, $T(x_w,x_u)\subset X_w^j\setminus\partial X_w^j$, $x_{w2},\dots,x_{wN_w}\in X_w^j\setminus\partial X_w^j$, $\Gamma_n[p_w,p_u]\subset \mathcal{G}_{\delta_{Z_w}}$ where $Z_w\in {\bf X}^n$ such that $\delta_{Z_w}=\min_{W\in{\bf X}^n,W\cap X_w^j\neq\emptyset}\delta_W$,
and $\operatorname{diam} \Gamma_n[p_w,p_u]\simeq_{C_T,C}\delta_{Z_w}$.
\end{enumerate} 
\item (2-sidedness property.) Suppose that $w,u \in \mathscr{W}$ are distinct with ${|u|}\leq {|w|}$
and $x_w=x_u$. Let $0<r\leq \min\{\delta_{X_w^1},\delta_{X_w^2}\}$, and let $p_{r},q_{r}\in\partial B(x_w,r)\cap T$ such that $x_w\in T(p_{r},q_{r})\subset B(x_w,r)$. Denote by $\sigma_w$ and $\sigma_u$ the components of $B(x_w,r)\cap\zeta_{w}$ and $B(x_w,r)\cap\zeta_{u}$, respectively, that contain $x_w$. Then $\sigma_w$, $\sigma_u$ are in different components of $B(x_w,r)\setminus T[p_{r},q_{r}]$. Last, if $w$ is not initial subword of $u$, then
$\zeta_w\cap\zeta_u = \{x_w\}$.                                                
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end prop-3 -->
<!-- rem-2: body_md -->
By (iv) of Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}, if $X\in{\bf X}^n$ and $Y\in{\bf X}^{n+1}$ with $Y\subset X$, then $2^{-k_X}\simeq_{C,C_T} 2^{-k_Y}$ which gives $0<k_Y-k_X\lesssim_{C,C_T} 1$. Moreover, by (viii) of Lemma \ref{lem:properties of BM}, $\lim_{n\to \infty} \sup_{X\in {\bf X}^n} \delta_{X} = 0$.
<!-- end rem-2 -->
<!-- lem-20: body_md -->
\label{lem:def of thickening of Tm,eta}
For each $n\in\mathbb{N}$, let $E_n=\bigcup_{X\in{\bf X}^n}\mathscr{T}_{\delta_X}(X)$. 
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item The boundary $\partial E_n$ consists of finitely many mutually disjoint Jordan curves, each of which is a subset of $\mathcal{G}_{\delta_n}$ where $\delta_n=\min_{X\in{\bf X}^n}\delta_X$. 
\item There exists a Jordan domain $\Omega_n$ such that $\partial\Omega_n\subset \partial E_n$ and $T\subset \mathrm{int} E_n\subset \Omega_n$.
\item If $m>n$, then  $\overline{\Omega_{m}}\subset \Omega_{n}$.
\item If $X,Y$ are disjoint $n$-tiles, then $$\operatorname{dist}(\mathscr{T}_{\delta_X}(X),\mathscr{T}_{\delta_Y}(Y))\geq C^{-1}\max\{\delta_X,\delta_Y\}.$$
\item The set $E_n$ is $(2^6C_TC)$-bounded turning.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-20 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Fix $n\in\mathbb{N}$. Note that $$T=\bigcup_{X\in\bf{X}^n}X\subset \bigcup_{X\in{\bf X}^n}\mathscr{T}_{\delta_X}(X).$$
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
By \eqref{eq:def of delta X}, $\mathcal{G}_{\delta_X} \subset \mathcal{G}_{\delta_n}$. Claims (i) and (ii) follow now from Lemma \ref{lem:properties of thickening} and the definition of $E_n$.

To show (iii), let $m>n$. By Lemma \ref{lem:properties of thickening}(v) $E_m \subset E_n$ which gives $\Omega_{m}\subset \Omega_{n}$. Let now $x\in \partial \Omega_{n}$
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                
and $y\in \partial \Omega_{m}$. By (i), $x\in\mathscr{T}_{\delta_X}(X)$ and $y\in \mathscr{T}_{\delta_Y}(Y)$ where $X$ is an $n$-tile and $Y$ is an $m$-tile. By Lemma \ref{lem:properties of thickening}(ii) there exists $w_Y\in Y$ such that $|w_Y-y|<8\delta_Y$. Moreover, by Lemma \ref{lem:properties of BM}(v), $Y$ is subset to a unique $n$-tile $Y'$. Since $x\in \partial \Omega_n$ it follows from (i) that $x\notin\mathrm{int}(\mathscr{T}_{\delta_{Y'}}(Y'))$. In conjunction with \eqref{eq:def of delta X} and Lemma \ref{lem:properties of thickening}(ii) we have
$$|x-y|\geq |x-w_Y|-|w_Y-y|\geq \delta_{Y'}-8\delta_Y>\delta_{Y'}-16C^{-1}\delta_{Y'}>0.$$
Thus, $\partial \Omega_n\cap \partial\Omega_m=\emptyset$, and hence $\overline{\Omega_m}\subset\Omega_n$.

To show (iv), let $X,Y$ be disjoint $n$-tiles. Let also $x\in\mathscr{T}_{\delta_X}(X),y\in \mathscr{T}_{\delta_Y}(Y)$. Without loss of generality suppose that $\delta_X\geq \delta_Y$. Let $w_x\in X, w_y\in Y$ be the points given by Lemma \ref{lem:properties of thickening}(ii) such that $|w_x-x|<8\delta_X$ and $|w_y-y|<8\delta_Y$. Since $X\cap Y=\emptyset$ it follows that $w_x\neq w_y$. Then, by \eqref{eq:def of delta X} and Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}(iii) we have
\begin{align*}
|x-y|\geq |w_x-w_y|-|w_x-x|-|w_y-y|\geq \operatorname{dist}(X,Y)-8\delta_X-8\delta_Y&\geq \frac{\operatorname{diam} X}{C}-16\delta_X\\ &>\frac{\delta_X}{C}. 
\end{align*}

To show (v), let $x_1,x_2\in E_n$. For each $i\in \{1,2\}$, there exists $X_i\in{\bf X}^n$ such that $x_i\in \mathscr{T}_{\delta_{X_i}}(X_i)$ and we may assume that $\operatorname{diam}{X_1}\leq \operatorname{diam}{X_2}$ which implies $\delta_{X_1}\leq \delta_{X_2}$. By Lemma \ref{lem:properties of thickening}(v) there exists $x_i'\in X_i$ such that $[x_i,x_i']\subset \mathscr{T}_{\delta_{X_i}}(X_i)$ and $|x_i-x_i'|< 9\delta_{X_i}$. We consider two cases. 

\emph{Case 1.} Suppose that $|x_1-x_2|<\delta_{X_1}$. For $i=1,2$, let $S_{i}\in\Sigma_{\delta_{X_{i}}}$ with $x_i\in S_{X_i}\subset \mathscr{T}_{\delta_{X_i}}(X_i)$. By \eqref{eq:def of delta X}, $\delta_{X_1}$ divides $\delta_{X_2}$ and therefore there exists $S'\in \Sigma_{\delta_{X_1}}$ such that $x_2\in S'\subset S_{2}$. Since $|x_1-x_2|<\delta_{X_1}$, we have that $S_{1}\cap S'\neq\emptyset$. If $S',S_{1}$ share a side, then $S'\cup S_{1}$ is a convex set and $[x_1,x_2]\subset E_n$. If $S',S_{1}$ do not share a side, then they share a corner $w$ and $\gamma=[x,w]\cup[w,y]\subset E_n$ and $\operatorname{diam} \gamma=|x_1-x_2|$.

\emph{Case 2.} Suppose that $|x_1-x_2| \geq \delta_{X_{1}}$. We consider two subcases.

\emph{Case 2.1.} Suppose that $X_1\cap X_2 \neq \emptyset$. 
                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            
Set $\sigma:=[x_1,x_1']\cup T[x_1',x_2']\cup [x_2',x_2]$. By (i), $\sigma\subset E_n$ and by the bounded turning property of $T$ and Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}(ii), as in Case 2.1,
\begin{align*}
\operatorname{diam}(\sigma)&\leq 9\delta_{X_{1}}+C_T|x_1'-x_2'|+9\delta_{X_{2}}\\ &\leq 9\delta_{X_1}+C_T(|x_1-x_2|+9\delta_{X_1}+18C\delta_{X_1})+18C\delta_{X_1}\\ &\leq 2^6CC_T|x_1-x_2|.
\end{align*}

\emph{Case 2.2}. Suppose that $X_1\cap X_2 = \emptyset$. Then, by Definition \ref{def:tile decomposition}(iii), 
\[ |x_1'-x_2'| \geq C^{-1}\operatorname{diam}{X_2} \geq 36\delta_{X_2}\]
and, setting $\sigma:=[x_1,x_1']\cup T[x_1',x_2']\cup [x_2',x_2]$,
\[ |x_1-x_2| \geq |x_1'-x_2'| - 9(\delta_{X_1} + \delta_{X_2}) \geq \frac12|x_1'-x_2'| \]
while
\[ \operatorname{diam}{\sigma} \leq 9\delta_{X_{1}}+C_T|x_1'-x_2'|+9\delta_{X_{2}}\leq 2C_T|x_1'-x_2'|. \qedhere\]
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lem-21: body_md -->
\label{lem:arzela curves} 
There exists a family of $C_T$-John arcs
\[ \{\gamma_{x,k}^i : x\in {\bf W}^*, k\geq \operatorname{\mathtt{L}}(x), \, i\in\{1,2\}\}\]
so that following are true for all $x\in{\bf W}^*$, $l\geq k\geq \operatorname{\mathtt{L}}(x)$, and $i\in\{1,2\}$.
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item One endpoint of $\gamma_{x,k}^i$ is $x$, the other is on $\Gamma_k$, and the rest is in $\Omega_k \setminus T$.
\item The arc $\gamma_{x,l}^i$ is the closure of the component of $\gamma_{x,k}^i\cap\Omega_{l}$ that contains $x$.
\item We have $\ell(\gamma_{x,k}^i)\leq 16CC_T\min\{\delta_{X_{1,x}^{k}},\delta_{X_{2,x}^{k}}\}$. In particular, $$\gamma_{x,n}^i\subset B(x,(2C)^{-1}r_{x,k+1})\subset B(x,r_{x,k})$$
where
\[ r_{x,k}=2^{14}C^4C^2_{T}\min\{\delta_{X_{x,k}^{1}},\delta_{X_{x,k}^{2}}\}. \]
\item[(iv)] We have
\begin{align*}
\overline{B}(x,r_{x,k})\cap \Gamma_k&\subset \mathscr{T}_{\delta_{X_{x,k}^1}}(X_{x,k}^1)\cup \mathscr{T}_{\delta_{X_{x,k}^2}}(X_{x,k}^2).
\end{align*}
\item[(v)] Let $0<r\leq r_{x,k}$ and $p_{r},q_{r}\in\partial B(x,r)\cap T$ such that $x\in T(p_{r},q_{r})\subset B(x,r)$. Denote by $U_{x,r}^1,U_{x,r}^2$ the connected components of $B(x,r)\setminus T[p_{r},q_{r}]$. The connected component of $B(x,r)\cap\gamma_{x,k}^i$ that contains $x$ lies in $U_{x,r}^i\cup\{x\}$. In particular, $\gamma_{x,k}^i\subset U_{x,r_{x,k}}^i\cup\{x\}$ and $\gamma_{x,k}^1\cap\gamma_{x,k}^2=\{x\}$. 
\item[(vi)] If $B_{x}^1,B_{x}^2$ are the components of $T\setminus\{x\}$, then there exist Jordan domains $O_1,O_2 \subset \Omega_k$ such that $B_{x}^i\subset O_i$, 
\[ \gamma_{x,k}^1\cup \gamma_{x,k}^2\cup\Gamma_{k}=\partial O_1\cup \partial O_2, \quad\text{and}\quad\partial O_1\cap \partial O_2=\gamma_{x,k}^1\cup \gamma_{x,k}^2.\]
\item[(vii)] If $y\in {\bf W}^*\setminus\{x\}$, $m\geq \operatorname{\mathtt{L}}(y)$, $j\in\{1,2\}$, and $t=\max\{m+1,k+1\}$ then 
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{dist}(\gamma_{x,k}^i,\gamma_{y,m}^j) \geq(2C)\max\{ \delta_{X_{x,t}^{1}},\delta_{X_{x,t}^2}, \delta_{X_{y,t}^1},\delta_{X_{y,t}^2}\}
\end{equation*}
\item[(viii)] If $i'\in\{1,2\}\setminus\{i\}$ and $\omega_{x,k}^{i'}$ is the subarc of $\gamma_{x,k}^{i'}$ in $\overline{\Omega_{k}}\setminus\Omega_{k+1}$ with one endpoint in $\Gamma_{k}$, then
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{dist}(\gamma_{x,k}^i,\omega_{x,k}^{i'})\geq (2C)^{-1}\max\{\delta_{X_{x,k+1}^{1}},\delta_{X_{x,k+1}^{2}}\}. 
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-21 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
For each $x\in{\bf W}^*$ and $m\geq \operatorname{\mathtt{L}}(x)$, write 
\[ R_{x,m}=\frac14\min\{\operatorname{diam} X_{x,m}^1,\operatorname{diam} X_{x,m}^2\}.\] 
By Lemma \ref{lem:properties of BM}(viii), $R_{x,m}\to0$ as $m\to\infty$. 

Fix $x\in{\bf W}^*$, let $n=\operatorname{\mathtt{L}}(x)$, and let $B_{x}^1,B_{x}^2$ be the components of $T\setminus \{x\}$. For $i=1,2$
                                           
we can find a point $p_{x}^i \in B_{x}^i\cap(\mathbb{R}^2\setminus\overline{B}(x,R_{x,n}))$. Let also $p_{x,m}^i$ be the first point when the arc $T[x,p_{x}^i]$ meets $\partial B(x,R_{x,m})$ as we travel from $x$ to $p_{x}^i$. Then the set $B(x,R_{x,m})\setminus T[p_{x,m}^1,p_{x,m}^2]$ has two connected components $W_{x,m}^1,W_{x,m}^2$. After relabeling, we may assume that $W_{x,m}^1\subset W_{x,m+1}^1$ and $W_{x,m}^2\subset W_{x,m+1}^2$ for all $m\geq n$. Note that $\bigcap_{m\geq n} \overline{W_{x,m}^i}=\{x\}$ for $i\in\{1,2\}$.

Fix, for $m\geq n$ and $i\in\{1,2\}$, points $y_{x,m}^i\in (W_{x,m}^i\setminus T)$. Fix $u\in\Gamma_1$ and set $B:=B(u,25)$. It follows from Lemma \ref{lem:properties of thickening}(ii) and the fact that $\operatorname{diam} T=1$ that $T\subset \overline{B}$ and $y_{x,m}^i\in \overline{B}$ for all $m\geq n$ and $i\in\{1,2\}$. Finally, fix $w_0\in\mathbb{R}^2\setminus\overline{B}$ such that $\operatorname{dist}(w_0,\overline{B})>50C_{T}$. 

By Lemma \ref{lem: Nakki vaisalla}, there exist families of arcs $\{\sigma_{x,m}^i : m\geq n, \, i=1,2\}$ such that each $\sigma_{x,m}^i$ has endpoints $w_0$ and $y_{x,m}^i$ and satisfy \eqref{eq:John} with constant $C_{T}$. Let $\tilde{y}_{x,m}^i$ be the first time that the arc $\sigma_{x,m}^i$ meets $\partial \overline{B}$ as we travel from $y_{x,m}^i$ to $w_0$. Then for all $z\in\sigma_{x,m}^i[y_{x,m}^i,\tilde{y}_{x,m}^i]\subset \overline{B}$ we have that $\ell(\sigma_{x,m}^i[y_{x,m}^i,z])\leq \ell(\sigma_{x,m}^i[w_0,z])$ since otherwise 
$$\operatorname{dist}(w_0,\overline{B})\leq |w_0-z|\leq\ell(\sigma_{x,m}^i[w_0,z])\leq C_{T}\operatorname{dist}(z,T)\leq 50C_{T}$$ 
which is contradiction. Therefore, for each $i\in\{1,2\}$ we have 
$$\ell(\sigma_{x,m}^i[y_{x,m}^i,\tilde{y}_{x,m}^i])\leq C_{T}\operatorname{dist}(\tilde{y}_{x,m}^i,T)\leq 50C_{T}$$ 
which implies that the arcs $\{\sigma_{x,m}^i[y_{x,m}^i,\tilde{y}_{x,m}^i]\}_{m\geq n}$ are Lipschitz arcs with uniform bound on their lengths. By the Arzela-Ascoli Theorem, for $i=1,2$, there exist limit curves $\sigma_{x}^i$ connecting $x$ to a point $y_x^i\in\partial B$. 

Let $\gamma_{x}^i\subset\sigma_x^i$ be an arc that has $x$ and $y_{x}^i$ as endpoints. We claim that $\gamma_{x}^i$ is $C_{T}$-John arc. To see that, fix $z\in \gamma_{x}^i$. For each $m\geq n$, there exist $z_m\in\sigma_{x,m}^i[y_{x,m}^i,\tilde{y}_{x,m}^i]$ such that $z_m \to z$.
