Quasiconvexity of $\mathcal T^4$ therefore gives
\[\max\{\operatorname{diam}{\mathcal T_u^4},\operatorname{diam}{\mathcal T_v^4}\}\lesssim_{\epsilon} \operatorname{diam}{\mathcal T^4[x,y]}\lesssim_{\epsilon} |x-y|=\operatorname{dist}(\mathcal T_u^4,\mathcal T_v^4).\]
By quasiconformality of $f$, 
\[\max\{\operatorname{diam}{f(\mathcal T_u^4)},\operatorname{diam}{f(\mathcal T_v^4)}\}\lesssim_{K,\epsilon} \operatorname{dist}(f(\mathcal T_u^4),f(\mathcal T_v^4)). \]

For (7), let $u\in A_4^n$ and $v\in A_4^{n+1}$ with $\mathcal{T}^4_{v}\cap \mathcal{T}^4_{u}\neq\emptyset$. If $\mathcal{T}^4_{v^\uparrow}=\mathcal{T}^4_{u}$, then $\mathcal{T}^4_{v}\simeq_{m,\epsilon}\mathcal{T}^4_{u}$. If $\mathcal{T}^4_{v^\uparrow}\neq \mathcal{T}^4_{u}$, then $\mathcal{T}^4_{v^\uparrow}\cap \mathcal{T}^4_{u}\neq\emptyset$, and by (5)
\[ \operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{T}^4_u) \simeq_{m,\epsilon} \operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{T}^4_{v^\uparrow}) \simeq_{m,\epsilon} \operatorname{diam}(\mathcal{T}^4_v).\]
In either case, by quasiconformality of $f$, $\operatorname{diam}{f(\mathcal{T}^4_{v})}\simeq_{K,\varepsilon}\operatorname{diam}{f(\mathcal{T}^4_{u})}$.

For (8), note that by quasiconformality of $f$, there exists $\rho\in (0,1)$ such that if $\mathcal{T}^4_u \cap \mathcal{T}^4_u \neq \emptyset$ and $\operatorname{diam}{\mathcal{T}^4_u} \leq \rho \operatorname{diam}{\mathcal{T}^4_v}$, then $\operatorname{diam}{f(\mathcal{T}^4_u)} \leq \frac12 \operatorname{diam}{f(\mathcal{T}^4_v)}$. Let $c$ be the constant from (5). Choose $k_0\in\mathbb{N}$ such that $(1/2)^{k_0}c < \rho$. Let $n\in\mathbb{N}_0$, $k\geq k_0$, $u\in A_4^{n}$, $v\in A_4^{n+k_0}$, and assume that $\mathcal{T}^4_u\cap \mathcal{T}^4_v \neq \emptyset$. Let $v'\in A_n^4$ and $v'' \in A^{k}_4$ such that $v=v'v''$. Noting that $1/2$ is the biggest scaling factor of the similarities $\psi_1,\dots,\psi_4$,
\[ \operatorname{diam}{\mathcal{T}^4_{v}} \leq (1/2)^k\operatorname{diam}{\mathcal{T}^4_{v'}} \leq (1/2)^kc\operatorname{diam}{\mathcal{T}^4_u} < \rho \operatorname{diam}{\mathcal{T}^4_u}.\]

Suppose now that $T$ admits a decomposition $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$ satisfying (1)--(8). Set $X_{\varepsilon}=T$ and write ${\bf X}^1 =\{X_1,\dots,X_4\}$ where tiles are enumerated according to the counterclockwise orientation. Suppose now that for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$ we have labeled $\bigcup_{m=0}^n{\bf X}^m = \{X_w : w\in \bigcup_{m=0}^nA_4^m\}$ so that 
\begin{enumerate}
\item $X_w \subset X_{w^{\uparrow}}$ if $w\in A_4^m$ and $m\in \{1,\dots,n\}$,
\item $\operatorname{card}{\partial X_w}=2$ if and only if $\operatorname{card}{\partial \mathcal{T}_w^4} =2$,
\item $X_w\cap X_u \neq \emptyset$ for $w,u \in \bigcup_{m=0}^nA_4^m$ if and only if $\mathcal{T}^4_w\cap \mathcal{T}^4_u \neq \emptyset$.
\end{enumerate}

Assume first that for $w\in A^n_4$ we have $\operatorname{card}{\partial X_w}=1$. Then there exists unique $u\in A^n_4\setminus\{w\}$ such that $X_w\cap X_u \neq \emptyset$. Write $X_w = X_{w1}\cup \cdots \cup X_{w4}$ enumerated according to the counterclockwise orientation of branches and so that $X_{wi} \cap X_u \neq \emptyset$ if and only if $\mathcal{T}_{wi}^4 \cap \mathcal{T}^4_u \neq \emptyset$.

Assume now that for $w\in A^n_4$ we have $\operatorname{card}{\partial X_w}=2$. Let $b_{X_w}$ be the branch point at which $X_w$ is subdivided by (2). Since the boundary points of an arc tile lie in opposite branches at $b_{X_w}$ (by (3)), we may label the four children of $X_w$ according to the four children of $\mathcal T^4_w$ so that the correspondence preserves containment, intersections, arc and leaf tiles, and opposite pairs. 

Proceeding inductively we define $X_w$ for every $w\in A_4^*$. By (8), for each $w\in A^{\mathbb{N}}_4$,
\[ \lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{diam}{X_{\{w\}}} = \lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{diam}{\mathcal{T}^4_{w\{n\}}} =0.\] Therefore, we can define surjective maps $\pi_T : A^{\mathbb{N}}_4 \to T$ and $\pi_{\mathcal{T}^4} : A^{\mathbb{N}}_4 \to \mathcal{T}^4$ by
\[ \pi_T(w)=\bigcap_{n\geq1}X_{w\{n\}},\qquad\pi_{\mathcal T^4}(w)= \bigcap_{n\geq1}\mathcal T^4_{w\{n\}}.\]
Since $X_w\cap X_u \neq \emptyset$ if and only if $\mathcal{T}^4_w\cap \mathcal{T}^4_u \neq \emptyset$, we have that $\pi_T(w)=\pi_T(u)$ if and only if $\pi_{\mathcal T^4}(w) = \pi_{\mathcal T^4}(u)$. Consequently, there exists a bijection $h:T\to\mathcal T^4$ such that $h(\pi_T(w))=\pi_{\mathcal T^4}(w)$. The function $h$ also satisfies $h(X_w)=\mathcal T_w^4$ for all $w\in A_4^*$. Since $\{\mathbf X^n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$ is a quasivisual approximation of $T$, and $\{\{\mathcal{T}^4_w:w\in A^n_4\}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$ is a a quasivisual approximation of $\mathcal{T}^4$, by \cite[Proposition 2.7]{BM22} $h$ is a quasisymmetry.

In particular, $h$ is a homeomorphism and hence preserves the valence of branch points. Since every branch point of $\mathcal T^4$ is $4$-valent, the same is true for $T$. Moreover, every branch point of $\mathcal T^4$ occurs as the subdivision point of some tile $\mathcal T_w^4$. Since $h(X_w)=\mathcal T_w^4$ and the correspondence preserves the four children, we have $ h(b_{X_w})=b_{\mathcal T_w^4}.$ It follows that every branch point of $T$ occurs as some $b_{X_w}$.

Finally, the labeling of tiles $X_w$ was chosen so that the cyclic order of the four branches at each $b_{X_w}$ agrees with that of the corresponding branch point of $\mathcal T^4$. Hence $h$ is branch-orientation-preserving. By Theorem \ref{thm: intro 2 quant.}(i), $h$ extends to a global quasiconformal homeomorphism of $\mathbb R^2$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- lem-26: body_md -->
\label{lem:semigeodesic Vicsek}
The Vicsek tree $\mathbb{V}$ is semi-geodesic.
<!-- end lem-26 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
It is known that the geodesic metric on $\mathbb{V}$ is bi-Lipschitz equivalent to the restriction of the Euclidean metric to $\mathbb{V}$; see \cite[Section 2.2]{BaudoinChen1}. Hence $\mathbb{V}$ is quasiconvex.

It remains to verify the second condition. Let
\[L=[-1-i,1+i]\cup[-1+i,1-i]\]
be the union of the two diagonals of $\mathbb{V}$, and for $n\geq 0$ set
\[\Gamma_n:=\bigcup_{|w|=n}\phi_w(L).\]
Then each $\Gamma_n$ is a finite tree consisting of line segments, $\Gamma_n\subset \mathbb{V}$, and $\bigcup_n\Gamma_n$ is dense in $V$.

Let $p\in \mathbb{V}\setminus\mathcal L(\mathbb{V})$. Choose points $x_1,x_2$ in two distinct components of $\mathbb{V}\setminus\{p\}$ and in $\bigcup_n\Gamma_n$. For sufficiently large $n$ we have $x_1,x_2\in\Gamma_n$. By uniqueness of arcs, $ \mathbb{V}[x_1,x_2]=\Gamma_n[x_1,x_2],$ so $\mathbb{V}[x_1,x_2]$ is a polygonal arc containing $p$. If $p$ is not a corner of this arc, then a sufficiently short subarc containing $p$ is a line segment. If $p$ is a corner, then $p=\phi_w(0)$ for some word $w$. Since $\phi_w([-1-i,1+i])\subset\mathbb V$, this line segment contains $p$ in its interior. Thus, in either case, $p$ lies in the interior of a line segment contained in $\mathbb V$, proving the second condition in the definition of a semi-geodesic tree.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- prop-6: body_md -->
\label{prop: Vicsek is QC with T^4}
The Vicsek tree is quasiconformally equivalent to $\mathcal{T}^4$.
<!-- end prop-6 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
For each branch point $p$ of $\mathbb V$, set
$$\operatorname{gen}(p):= \min\{|w|:w\in A^*,\ p=\phi_w(0)\}.$$
                                                     

We define three types of tiles on $\mathbb{V}$. First, any image of the set
\[ D_{\dagger}:=\{0\}\cup\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathbb V_{5^{(k)}1}\]
under a similarity is called a $\dagger$-tile. Note that $\mathbb{V}$ is the union of 4 $\dagger$-tiles which intersect exactly at 0 and are isometric to each other. Second, $D_{\dagger}$ is a union of 4 subtrees that intersect at $\psi_1(0)$; the three of them are $\dagger$-tiles isometric to each other and the fourth is denoted by $D_{\ddagger}$. Any image of $D_{\ddagger}$ under a similarity is called a $\ddagger$-tile. Third, $D_{\ddagger}$ is a union of 4 subtrees that intersect exactly at $\psi_{13}(0)$; one is a $\ddagger$-tile, two are $\dagger$-tiles that are opposite and isometric to each other, and the third is denoted by $D_{\times}$. Any image of $D_{\times}$ under a similarity is called a $\times$-tile. Finally, $D_{\times}$ is a union of four subtrees that intersect exactly at $\psi_{51}(0)$; two are $\ddagger$tiles which are opposite and isometric and two are $\dagger$-tiles that are opposite and isometric. The boundaries of these three models, relative to $\mathbb V$, are $\partial D_\dagger=\{0\}$, $\partial D_\ddagger=\{0,\phi_1(0)\}$, and $\partial D_\times=\{0,\phi_{13}(0)\}$. 

\begin{figure}[h]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.31\textwidth]{daggervicsek.png}
    \hfill
    \includegraphics[width=0.31\textwidth]{doubledaggervicsek.png}
    \hfill
    \includegraphics[width=0.31\textwidth]{crossvicsek.png}
    \caption{The three model tiles: a $\dagger$-tile, a $\ddagger$-tile,
    and a $\times$-tile.}
    \label{fig:vicsek-model-tiles}
\end{figure}

Let ${\bf X}^0 = \{\mathbb{V}\}$ and let ${\bf X}^1 = \{X_1,\dots,X_4\}$ where each $X_i$ is a $\dagger$-tile isometric to $D_{\dagger}$ and all $X_i$ intersect at $0$. Assume now that we have defined ${\bf X}^n = \{X_w : w\in A_4^n\}$ for some $n\in\mathbb{N}$ so that the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(a)] Each $X_w$ is either a $\dagger$-tile, or a $\ddagger$-tile, or a $\times$-tile.
\item[(b)] If $X_w$ is a $\dagger$-tile, then $\partial X_w$ contains exactly one branch point.
\item[(c)] If $X_w$ is a $\ddagger$-tile, then $\partial X_w$ contains exactly two points $\{p,q\}$, both branch points. One of them satisfies $p\in \partial X_{w^{\uparrow}}$ and $\operatorname{gen}(p)\leq \operatorname{gen}(q)$.
\item[(d)] If $X_w$ is a $\times$-tile, then $\partial X_w$ contains exactly two points $\{p,q\}$, both branch points. They satisfy $\operatorname{gen}(p)< \operatorname{gen}(q)$.
\end{enumerate}
Fix $w\in A_4^n$. We consider the following instances. 
\begin{enumerate}
\item[(i)] If $X_w$ is a $\dagger$-tile with $\partial X_w=\{p\}$, then $X_w$ consists of 3 isometric $\dagger$-tiles, two of which are opposite, and one $\ddagger$-tile,all intersecting at $b_w\in X\setminus\{p\}$ with $\operatorname{gen}(b_w)=\operatorname{gen} (p)+1$.
\item[(ii)] If $X_w$ is a $\ddagger$-tile with $\partial X_w=\{p,q\}$ and $p\in \partial X_{w^\uparrow}$, then $\operatorname{gen}(p)\le \operatorname{gen}(q)$ and $X_w$ consists of a $\times$-tile with $p$ in its boundary, $2$ opposite isometric $\dagger$-tiles and $1$ $\ddagger$-tile, all intersecting at a branch point $b_w\in X\setminus\{p,q\}$ with $\operatorname{gen}(b_{w})=\operatorname{gen}(q)+1$.
\item[(iii)] If $X_w$ is a $\times$-tile with $\partial X_w=\{p,q\}$, then, $\operatorname{gen}(p)<\operatorname{gen}(q)$, and $X_w$ consists of 2 isometric opposite $\dagger$-tiles and $2$ isometric opposite $\ddagger$-tiles, all intersecting at a branch point $b_w$ with $\operatorname{gen} (b_w)=\operatorname{gen}(q)$.
\end{enumerate}
The new collection ${\bf X}^{n+1}$ consisting of all new tiles produced as above, satisfies (a)--(d). A simple induction now shows that properties (1)--(4) and the intersection assertion in (5) of Proposition \ref{prop: uniformization of T^4} hold.

We next verify the comparability of intersecting tiles. For a similarity copy $X$ of a model tile, let $s(X)$ denote its similarity depth, so that $\operatorname{diam} X=3^{-s(X)}d_{\tau(X)}$, where $\tau(X)\in\{\dagger,\ddagger,\times\}$ is its type and $d_\tau$ is the diameter of the corresponding model. The four initial $\dagger$-tiles have depth $0$. The defining similarities give the following parent--child increments:
\[
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}\label{eq:table for Vicsek}
\text{parent}&\text{child type}&\text{multiplicity}
&s(X')-s(X)\\ \hline
\dagger&\dagger&3&1\\
\dagger&\ddagger&1&0\\
\ddagger&\times&1&0\\
\ddagger&\dagger&2&2\\
\ddagger&\ddagger&1&1\\
\times&\ddagger&2&1\\
\times&\dagger&2&2
\end{array}
\tag{1.1}
\]

Let $Z,W\in\mathbf X^N$ distinct such that $Z\cap W\neq\emptyset$, and let $Y$ be their longest common ancestor. Write
\[Y=Z_0\supset Z_1\supset\cdots\supset Z_k=Z\quad\text{and}\quad Y=W_0\supset W_1\supset\cdots\supset W_k=W. \]
Their intersection is $\{p\}$, where $p=b_Y$, and $Z_i\cap W_i=\{p\}$ for $1\leq i\leq k$. Thus each subsequent tile in either chain is the unique child containing $p$. If $k=1$, \eqref{eq:table for Vicsek} gives $|s(Z)-s(W)|\leq2$; when $Y=\mathbb V$, the two children have equal depth. Suppose henceforth that $k\geq2$. We consider the following cases.

\emph{Case 1: $Y=\mathbb V$.} The tiles $Z_1,W_1$ are isometric $\dagger$-tiles. Their children containing $p=0$ are $\ddagger$-tiles of equal depth, in which $p$ has smaller generation than the other boundary point. The two chains thereafter have identical similarity-depth increments. Hence $s(Z)=s(W)$.

\emph{Case 2: $Y$ is a $\dagger$-tile.}
Write $\operatorname{gen}(\partial Y)=n$. Then $\operatorname{gen}(p)=n+1$. A $\dagger$-child of $Y$ has depth $s(Y)+1$, and its child containing $p$ is a $\ddagger$-tile of the same depth. The $\ddagger$-child of $Y$ has depth $s(Y)$ and boundary generations $n,n+1$, with $p$ of larger generation. Its child containing $p$ is therefore its $\ddagger$-child, of depth $s(Y)+1$. Consequently, regardless of which two children $Z_1,W_1$ occur, $Z_2,W_2$ are $\ddagger$-tiles of depth $s(Y)+1$, with boundary generations $n+1,n+2$ and $p$ of smaller generation. Their subsequent increments agree, and $s(Z)=s(W)$.

\emph{Case 3: $Y$ is a $\times$-tile.} Write $\partial Y=\{a,q\}$, where $\operatorname{gen}(a)=m<n=\operatorname{gen}(q)$. Then $\operatorname{gen}(p)=n$. Each $\dagger$-child of $Y$ has depth $s(Y)+2$, and its child containing $p$ is a $\ddagger$-tile of the same depth. The two $\ddagger$-children of $Y$ have depth $s(Y)+1$ and boundary generations $m,n$ and $n,n$. In the first, $p$ has larger generation. In the second, $p$ is the newly created boundary point, rather than the inherited one. Thus, in both cases, the child containing $p$ is the $\ddagger$-child, of depth $s(Y)+2$. It follows that $Z_2,W_2$ are $\ddagger$-tiles of equal depth, with boundary generations $n,n+1$ and $p$ of smaller generation. Their subsequent increments agree, so $s(Z)=s(W)$.

\emph{Case 4: $Y$ is a $\ddagger$-tile.} Let $n$ be the larger boundary generation of $Y$, allowing the two generations to be equal. Then $\operatorname{gen}(p)=n+1$. Each $\dagger$-child has depth $s(Y)+2$ and contributes a $\ddagger$-child containing $p$ at the same depth. This latter tile has boundary generations $n+1,n+2$. The $\ddagger$-child of $Y$ has depth $s(Y)+1$, with $p$ of larger boundary generation. Its child containing $p$ is a $\ddagger$-tile of depth $s(Y)+2$, again with boundary generations $n+1,n+2$. Finally, the $\times$-child has depth $s(Y)$. Its child containing $p$ is a $\ddagger$-tile of depth $s(Y)+1$, with both boundary generations equal to $n+1$. In this tile, $p$ is the boundary point inherited from its parent. Therefore $Z_2,W_2$ are $\ddagger$-tiles whose depths differ by at most one. In each, $p$ either has smaller generation or is the inherited boundary point in the equal-generation case. The two alternating-pattern observations apply, so all subsequent similarity-depth increments agree. Hence $|s(Z)-s(W)|\leq1$.

Combining these cases with $k=1$ gives $|s(Z)-s(W)|\leq2$. Set $d_-:=\min\{d_\dagger,d_\ddagger,d_\times\}$ and $d_+:=\max\{d_\dagger,d_\ddagger,d_\times\}$. Then
\[ \left(9\frac{d_+}{d_-}\right)^{-1} \leq \frac{\operatorname{diam} Z}{\operatorname{diam} W}= 3^{s(W)-s(Z)}
\frac{d_{\tau(Z)}}{d_{\tau(W)}} \leq9\frac{d_+}{d_-}.\]
This proves the diameter comparison in (5).

We show (6). For each tile $X$ with two boundary points, denote by $I_X$ its core, namely the arc joining these points. Since such tiles are similarity copies of $D_\ddagger$ or $D_\times$, whose cores are nondegenerate, $\operatorname{diam} I_X\simeq_{\mathbb{V}} \operatorname{diam} X$.

Let $X,Y\in\mathbf X^n$ be disjoint, and suppose that $\operatorname{diam} X\geq\operatorname{diam} Y$. Choose $x\in X$ and $y\in Y$ with $|x-y|=\operatorname{dist}(X,Y)$. After leaving $X$, the arc $\mathbb V[x,y]$ traverses a tile $Z\in\mathbf X^n$ adjacent to $X$. Since $X$ and $Y$ are disjoint, it enters and leaves $Z$ at distinct boundary points. Thus $Z$ is an arc tile and $I_Z\subset\mathbb V[x,y]$. Using bounded turning of $\mathbb V$ (see \cite[6.2]{DV}) and the comparison of intersecting tiles, we obtain
\[\operatorname{diam} X\simeq\operatorname{diam} Z\simeq\operatorname{diam} I_Z\leq\operatorname{diam} \mathbb V[x,y]\lesssim |x-y| =\operatorname{dist}(X,Y).\]

Property (7) follows from \eqref{eq:table for Vicsek}. Finally, we verify (8). Let $C_0$ be the comparability constant in (5), and choose $k\in\mathbb N$ sufficiently large that
\[C_0\frac{d_+}{d_-}3^{-k}\leq\frac12.\]
Set $N=3k$. Suppose first that $Y\in {\bf X}^{n+N}, X\in{\bf X}^n$, and $Y\subset X$. Then by \eqref{eq:table for Vicsek} $s(Y)-s(X)\geq k,$ and hence
\[\operatorname{diam} Y\leq \frac{d_+}{d_-}3^{-k}\operatorname{diam} X.\]
If $Y\not\subset X$, it follows similarly by applying property (5).
This proves (8).
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- prop-7: body_md -->
\label{prop: J(f) is QC with T^4}
There exists $c\in \mathbb{C}$ such that the Julia set of $z^2 +c$ is quasiconformally equivalent to $\mathbb{V}$.
<!-- end prop-7 -->
<!-- cor-3: body_md -->
Any planar tree with a decomposition $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$ that satisfies (1)--(8) in Proposition \ref{prop: uniformization of T^4} is a Julia set of a UQR map.
<!-- end cor-3 -->
<!-- lem-27: body_md -->
\label{lem:valence in tree Julia set}
If $b\in J(f)$, then $\operatorname{Val} (f(b))=\operatorname{Val}(b)/d$ where $d$ is the local degree of $f$ at $b$.
<!-- end lem-27 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
Suppose that $d$ is the local degree of $f$ at $b$ and that $\operatorname{Val} f(b)=p$. Then there exist open neighborhoods $U$ of $f$ and $V$ of $f(b)$ such that $f:U\setminus\{b\}\to V\setminus\{f(b)\}$ is a $d$-sheeted covering map. 

Let $B_1,\dots,B_p$ be the closures of components of $J(f)\setminus \{f(b)\}$. For each $i\in \{1,\dots,p\}$ let $\gamma_i\subset V\cap \overline{B_i}$ be arcs emanating from $f(b)$. Then the lifting of these arcs $\widetilde{\gamma_{i,j}}\subset U$ emanate from $b$ and 
\[ (\widetilde{\gamma_{i,j}}\setminus\{b\})\cap (\widetilde{\gamma_{i,j'}}\setminus\{b\})=\emptyset\] 
for distinct $j, j'\in \{1,\dots,d\}$. Since $J(f)$ is a tree it follows that for each $i\in \{1,\dots,p\}$ the arcs $\widetilde{\gamma_{i,j}}$ are located in distinct branches of $b$. Moreover, if for some $i\in \{1,\dots,p\}$, $k,k'\in \{1,\dots,d\}$, the arcs  $\widetilde{\gamma_{i,k}},\widetilde{\gamma_{i',k'}}$ are located in the same branch of $b$, then there exists a common subarc $\alpha\subset \widetilde{\gamma_{i,k}}\cup\widetilde{\gamma_{i',k'}}$ emanating from $b$ that has two distinct images and hence contradiction. Thus, $\operatorname{Val}(b)\geq dp$. Denote now by $B_1,\dots, B_{pd}$ the associated branches of $\{\widetilde{\gamma_{i,j}}\}_{i,j}$. If $B$ is a branch of $b$ that is not one of $B_1,\dots, B_{pd}$, then let $\omega\subset U\cap B$ be an arc emanating from $b$. Since Julia sets are completely invariant $f(\omega)$ lies in one of the branches of $f(b)$. Thus, there exists a subarc of $f(\omega)$ that has $d+1$ preimages which is contradiction.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- lem-28: body_md -->
\label{lem:valence of J(f)}
    The Julia set $J(f)$ is a quadrivalent quasiconformal tree with 
    $$\mathcal{B}(J(f))=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}f^{-n}(0)$$
    with the convention that if $n=0$, then $f^{0}(0)=0$.
<!-- end lem-28 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
Since $J(f)$ is a tree and $f$ is semi-hyperbolic, by \cite[Theorem]{CJY} it is a quasiconformal tree. 

Let now $T=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}T_n$. Note that by \cite[Example 6.6]{Denavey} the branch points of $T$ are preimages of $0$. Since $c$ is not preimage of 0 and $c\in (-q,q)\subset T_1$, it follows that $c$ is a double point in $T$. Moreover, the local degree of $f$ at $0$ is $2$ and hence by Lemma \ref{lem:valence in tree Julia set} we have that $\operatorname{Val}_{T}(0)=4$. Let now $C_1,\ldots,C_4$ be the four components of $T\setminus\{0\}$. They lie in four distinct components of $J(f)\setminus\{0\}$. Indeed, if $x_i\in C_i$ and $x_j\in C_j$, then $x_i,x_j\in T_n$ for some $n$, and the unique arc $J(f)[x_i,x_j]$ coincides with the arc joining them in the subtree $T_n$, which contains $0$. Conversely, every component of $J(f)\setminus\{0\}$ is relatively open in $J(f)$, since $J(f)$ is a tree, and therefore intersects the dense set $T$. Thus there can be no fifth component, and $\operatorname{Val}_{J(f)}(0)=4$. Consequently, since $0$ is the only critical point of $f$, Lemma \ref{lem:valence in tree Julia set} gives that $\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}f^{-n}(0)\subset \mathcal{B}(J(f))$ and that for all $b\in \bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}f^{-n}(0)$, $\operatorname{Val}_{J(f)}(b)=4$.

Conversely, let $b$ be a branch point of $J(f)$. Choose three distinct components of $J(f)\setminus\{b\}$. Each is relatively open and therefore intersects the dense set $T=\bigcup_n T_n$. Choose one point in each component and then $N$ large enough that all three belong to $T_N$. By uniqueness of arcs, their joining arcs in $T_N$ coincide with those in $J(f)$, so $b$ is a branch point of $T_N$. By the finite-tree construction, $b$ is precritical. This proves the asserted description of $\mathcal B(J(f))$, and the valence assertion follows from the preceding argument.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- lem-29: body_md -->
\label{lem: properties of branches of J(f)}
Let $b\in\mathcal{B}(J(f))$. 
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item There exists a minimal $m\geq 1$ such that
\[b\in f^{-(m-1)}(0)\cap\mathcal{B}(T_m).\]
\item There are two opposite branches $N_{b,m}^+$ and $N_{b,m}^-$ of $b$ in $T_m$ such that
\[ N_{b,m}^+\cap T_{m-1}=N_{b,m}^-\cap T_{m-1}=\emptyset.\]
\item For every $n> m$ and $s\in\{+,-\}$, let $N_{b,n}^s$ be the unique branch of $b$ in $T_n$ containing $N_{b,m}^s$. Then the set
\[ N_b^s:= \overline{\bigcup_{n\geq m}N_{b,n}^s}\setminus\{b\} \]
is a branch of $b$ in $J(f)$. Moreover, $0\notin N_b^s$, and the branches $N_b^+$,$N_b^-$ are opposite of $b$ in $J(f)$.
\item After choosing the signs consistently, the map
\[ f^{m-1}\big|_{N_b^+\cup N_b^-}: N_b^+\cup N_b^- \to N_0^+\cup N_0^-\]
is a homeomorphism and for each $s\in\{+,-\}$, $N_b^s\cap \operatorname{post} (f)=\emptyset$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end lem-29 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
By Lemma \ref{lem:valence of J(f)} and the nestedness of $\{T_n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$ there exists a minimal $m\geq1$ such that $b\in f^{-(m-1)}(0)\cap\mathcal B(T_m).$ This gives (i).

To show (ii), let $I=[-q,c]$. Since
$f^{-1}(I)$ is the non-real arc through $0$, the two components $N_{0,1}^+$ and $N_{0,1}^-$ of $f^{-1}(I)\setminus\{0\}$ are opposite branches of $0$ in $T_1$. Now, it follows by construction that the two pullback arcs of $I$ under $f^m$ having $b$ as an endpoint are precisely the lifts under $f^{m-1}$ of $N_{0,1}^+$ and $N_{0,1}^-$. Their interiors are contained in $T_m\setminus T_{m-1}$, and hence they determine two branches $N_{b,m}^+$ and $N_{b,m}^-$ of $b$ in $T_m$ satisfying $N_{b,m}^s\cap T_{m-1}=\emptyset.$ Since $b,f(b),\ldots,f^{m-2}(b)$ are not critical points, $f^{m-1}$ is a local orientation-preserving homeomorphism at $b$. It therefore preserves the cyclic order of the four local branches. Since $N_{0,1}^+$ and $N_{0,1}^-$ are opposite at $0$, their lifts $N_{b,m}^+$ and $N_{b,m}^-$ are opposite at $b$.

To show (iii), for every $n> m$, let $N_{b,n}^s, s\in\{+,-\},$ denote the unique branch of $b$ in $T_n$ containing $N_{b,m}^s$. Since $T_n\subset T_{n+1}$, $N_{b,n}^s\subset N_{b,n+1}^s$. We claim that
$$N_b^s:=\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq m}N_{b,n}^s}\setminus\{b\}$$
is a branch of $b$ in $J(f)$.

Let $B_b^s$ denote the branch of $b$ in $J(f)$ containing $N_{b,m}^s$. Clearly, $\bigcup_{n\geq m}N_{b,n}^s\subset B_b^s$. For the reverse inclusion, let $x\in B_b^s$. Since $B_b^s$ is relatively open in $J(f)$ and $J(f)=\overline{\bigcup_{n\geq 0}T_n}$, there exist $x_k\in B_b^s\cap\bigcup_{n\geq 0}T_n$ such that $x_k\to x$. We may assume that $x_k\notin N_{b,m}^s$. For each $k$, fix $p_k\in N_{b,m}^s$ and choose $n_k\geq m$ sufficiently large so that $x_k,p_k\in T_{n_k}$. Since $x_k$ and $p_k$ belong to the same component of $J(f)\setminus\{b\}$, the arc $J(f)[x_k,p_k]$ does not contain $b$. Moreover, since $T_{n_k}$ is a subtree of $J(f)$ and $x_k,p_k\in T_{n_k}$, we have $J(f)[x_k,p_k]= T_{n_k}[x_k,p_k]$. Thus $x_k$ and $p_k$ belong to the same branch of $b$ in $T_{n_k}$. Since $p_k\in N_{b,m}^s\subset N_{b,n_k}^s$, it follows that $x_k\in N_{b,n_k}^s$. Therefore, by the definition of $N_b^s$ and the fact that $x_k\to x\neq b$, we have $x\in N_b^s$. Hence $B_b^s=N_b^s$.

We also note that $0\notin N_b^s$. This is immediate when $b=0$, so suppose that $b\neq0$. Then $m\geq2$ and $0\in T_{m-1}$. By (ii), $N_{b,m}^s\cap T_{m-1}=\varnothing$. Fix $p\in N_{b,m}^s$. Thus $p$ and $0$ lie in distinct branches of $b$ in $T_m$, and consequently the arc $T_m[p,0]$ contains $b$. If $0\in N_{b,n}^s$ for some $n\geq m$, then, since $p\in N_{b,m}^s\subset N_{b,n}^s$, the arc $T_n[p,0]$ would not contain $b$. On the other hand, $p,0\in T_m$ and $T_m$ is a subtree of $T_n$, so uniqueness of arcs gives $T_n[p,0]=T_m[p,0]$, a contradiction. Hence $0\notin N_{b,n}^s$ for every $n\geq m$. Since $N_b^s$ is the branch of $b$ determined by these nested branches, it follows that $0\notin N_b^s$. Last, by nestedness of $\{T_n\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$ the branches $N_{b,n}^+,N_{b,n}^-$ are opposite in $T_n$, for $n>m$ and thus $N_b^+,N_b^-$ are opposite as well.

To show (iv), we show first the following elementary fact. For every $x\in J(f)$, $0\in J(f)[x,-x].$ Indeed, $f(-z)=f(z)$, and hence $J(f)$ is invariant under the involution $A(z)=-z.$ Therefore
$$A(J(f)[x,-x]) = J(f)[-x,x] = J(f)[x,-x].$$
The restriction of $A$ to the arc $J(f)[x,-x]$, which is homeomorphic to a closed interval, interchanges its two endpoints. Hence, it has a fixed point on this arc. The only fixed point of $A(z)=-z$ is $0$ and thus $0\in J(f)[-x,x]$. It follows in particular that if $K\subset J(f)$ is connected and $0\notin K$, then $f|_K$ is injective. Indeed, if $x,y\in K$, $x\neq y$, and $f(x)=f(y)$, then $y=-x$. Since a connected subset of a dendrite containing $x$ and $-x$ contains their unique arc, $J(f)[x,-x]\subset K$. Hence, $0\in K,$ which is contradiction.

We now claim that, for every branch point $p\neq 0$ and every sufficiently large $n$, $f(N_{p,n}^s)=N_{f(p),n-1}^s$, with the signs chosen consistently. Indeed, this holds at the first level at which $p$ appears by the definition. If it holds at level $n$, then every new arc added to $N_{p,n}^s$ is a lift of an arc added to $N_{f(p),n-1}^s$. Conversely, every such arc has a unique lift in $N_{p,n}^s$, since $0\notin N_{p,n}^s$ and hence $f$ is injective there. Thus the claim follows by induction.

Applying the claim successively to $b,f(b),\ldots,f^{m-2}(b)$ we obtain 
\[ f^{m-1}(N_{b,n}^s)=N_{0,n-m+1}^s.\] 
Passing to the increasing unions and using continuity gives $f^{m-1}(N_b^s)\subset N_0^s$. Conversely, let $y\in N_0^s$ and choose $y_k\in N_{0,n_k-m+1}^s$ with $y_k\to y$. Choose $x_k\in N_{b,n_k}^s$ with $f^{m-1}(x_k)=y_k$. By compactness of $\overline{N_b^s}$, after passing to a subsequence, $x_k\to x\in\overline{N_b^s}$. Since $y\neq0=f^{m-1}(b)$, we have $x\neq b$, and hence $x\in N_b^s$. Therefore $f^{m-1}(x)=y$, proving $f^{m-1}(N_b^s)=N_0^s$. Finally, by (ii) we have that $f^{m-1}$ is injective on $N_b^+\cup N_b^-$. Hence
\[f^{m-1}|_{N_b^+\cup N_b^-}:N_b^+\cup N_b^-\longrightarrow N_0^+\cup N_0^-\]
is a homeomorphism. 

It remains to verify that for each $s\in \{+,-\}$, $N_b^s\cap \operatorname{post} (f)=\emptyset$. By construction, $N_0^s\cap \operatorname{post} (f)=\emptyset$. Now, suppose that $x\in N_b^s\cap\operatorname{post}(f)$ for some $s\in\{+,-\}$.
Since $\operatorname{post}(f)$ is forward invariant, $f^{m-1}(x)\in\operatorname{post}(f).$
On the other hand, by the above
$f^{m-1}(x)\in N_0^s.$ Thus
$f^{m-1}(x)\in N_0^s\cap\operatorname{post}(f),$
which is impossible.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- lem-30: body_md -->
\label{lem:koebe}
Let $D,U$ be simply connected domains in $\mathbb{C}$ with $\overline{D} \subset U$ let $z_0\in D$. There exists a constant $C\geq1$, depending only on $D$, $U$, and $z_0$, such that every univalent map $g:U\to\mathbb C$ satisfies
\[ C^{-1}|g'(z_0)|\,|z-w| \leq |g(z)-g(w)| \leq C|g'(z_0)|\,|z-w| \]
for all $z,w\in D$. Consequently, for every compact set $E\subset D$,
\[ C^{-1}|g'(z_0)|\operatorname{diam} E \leq \operatorname{diam} g(E) \leq C|g'(z_0)|\operatorname{diam} E.\]
<!-- end lem-30 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
Since $\overline{D}\subset U$, the annular region separating $D$ from $\partial U$ has positive modulus. More precisely, there is $m>0$ such that $\operatorname{mod}(D,U)\geq m.$ McMullen's two-point distortion theorem \cite[Theorem 2.9]{McM94} therefore gives a constant $C=C(m)\geq1$ such that, for every univalent map $g:U\to\mathbb C$ and all $x,y,z\in D$,
\[C^{-1}|g'(x)|\leq\frac{|g(y)-g(z)|}{|y-z|}\leq C|g'(x)|.\]
Taking $x=z_0$ yields the desired estimate.

Now let $E\subset D$ be compact. The upper bound follows by applying the preceding estimate to arbitrary $y,z\in E$ and taking the supremum. Since $E$ is compact, there exist $y,z\in E$ such that $|y-z|=\operatorname{diam} E$. Applying the lower bound to this pair gives the lower bound.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
<!-- lem-31: body_md -->
\label{lem:koebe2}
There exist $r>0$, $C>0$, and $\lambda\in(0,1)$ such that, for every $z\in J(f)$, every $n\in\mathbb N$, and every component $W$ of $f^{-n}(B(z,r))$, we have $ \operatorname{diam} W\leq C\lambda^n$.
<!-- end lem-31 -->
<!-- proof-36: body_md -->
The only finite critical point is $0$ and it has a finite orbit, and hence nonrecurrent. Moreover, $f$ has no parabolic periodic points. Indeed, every cycle of immediate parabolic basins contains a critical point. Since the only finite critical point of $f$ is $0$, the existence of a parabolic cycle would imply that the orbit of $0$ converges to this cycle. This contradicts the above
orbit, which lands on the fixed point $-p$. Thus $f$ is semihyperbolic in the sense of \cite{CJY}. The exponential shrinking follows now from  a standard characterization of semihyperbolicity; see \cite{CJY} and also \cite{Miha}.
<!-- end proof-36 -->
<!-- proof-37: body_md -->
By Proposition \ref{prop: uniformization of T^4} and Proposition \ref{prop: Vicsek is QC with T^4}, it is enough to find a subdivision $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}$ of $J(f)$ that satisfies properties (1)--(8) of Proposition \ref{prop: uniformization of T^4}.

For each branch point $p$ of $J(f)$, set
\[\operatorname{gen}(p):=\min\{m\in\mathbb N_0:p\in f^{-m}(0)\}.\]
For $M\in\mathbb N_0$, let $ \Gamma_M:=f^{-M}([-q,q])$ and set
\[ V_M= \bigcup_{j=0}^{M-1}f^{-j}(0) \cup f^{-M}(\{-q,q\}).\]
We call the closure of a component of $\Gamma_M\setminus V_M$ an \emph{edge} of $\Gamma_M$. 

We first record a simple observation about these edges. If $E$ is an edge of $\Gamma_M$, then $f^M$ maps $E$ homeomorphically onto a real interval whose endpoints belong to $ \{-q,c,-p,p,q\}.$ Indeed, the interior of $E$ contains no critical point of $f^M$, while an endpoint of $E$ is either a branch point of $\Gamma_M$ or a leaf of $\Gamma_M$. In the former case, its image under $f^M$ belongs to $\{c,p,-p\}$, and in the latter case it belongs to $\{-q,q\}$. Let $a=\sqrt{-c}$. The first precritical points in the interiors of $[-q,c]$, $[c,-p]$, $[-p,p]$, $[p,q]$ are $-\sqrt{a-c}$, $-a$, $0$, $a$, respectively, and have generations $2$, $1$, $0$, $1$, respectively. Every other interval with endpoints in $\{-q,c,-p,p,q\}$ either contains one of these intervals or contains $0$. It follows that the first precritical point in the interior of $f^M(E)$ has generation at most two. Consequently, the first branch point introduced in the interior of $E$ belongs to $f^{-j}(0)$ for some $j\in \{M,M+1,M+2\}$.

We now define the subdivision $({\bf X}^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}_0}$. Set $\mathbf X^0=\{J(f)\}$ and let $\mathbf X^1=\{X_1,\ldots,X_4\}$ be the closures of the four branches of $J(f)$ at $0$. Thus $\partial X_i=\{0\}$ for every $i\in A_4$. Assume now that $\mathbf X^n=\{X_w:w\in A_4^n\}$ has been defined for some $n\in\mathbb N$ so that the following properties hold.
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item For every $w\in A_4^n$, $\operatorname{card}(\partial X_w)\in\{1,2\}$.
\item The set $E_w:=X_w\cap\Gamma_{m_w+1}$ is a closed edge of $\Gamma_{m_w+1}$, and $\partial X_w$ is contained in the endpoints of $E_w$.
\item If $\partial X_w=\{p_w\}$ then set $m_w=\operatorname{gen}(p_w)$. 
\item If $\partial X_w=\{p_w,q_w\}$, label the boundary points so that $\operatorname{gen}(q_w)<\operatorname{gen}(p_w)$ and set $m_w=\operatorname{gen}(p_w)$. 
\item We have $(X_w\setminus\partial X_w)\cap \bigcup_{j=0}^{m_w}f^{-j}(0)=\emptyset$.
\end{enumerate}

Fix $w\in A_4^n$, and let $b_w$ be the first branch point introduced in the interior of $E_w$ by the finite-tree construction. By the edge observation,
\[ \operatorname{gen}(b_w)\in \{m_w+1,m_w+2,m_w+3\}.\]
Let $X_{w1},\ldots,X_{w4}$ be the closures of the four components of $X_w\setminus\{b_w\}$, labeled in cyclic order. If $\partial X_w=\{p_w\}$, choose the labeling so that $\partial X_{w1}=\{p_w,b_w\}$; then $\partial X_{wj}=\{b_w\}$ for $j\in\{2,3,4\}$. If $\partial X_w=\{p_w,q_w\}$, the two boundary points lie in opposite branches at $b_w$. Choose the labeling so that $\partial X_{w1}=\{q_w,b_w\}$ and $\partial X_{w3}=\{p_w,b_w\}$; then $\partial X_{w2}=\partial X_{w4}=\{b_w\}$. At its first appearance, $b_w$ is the only branch point of $X_w\cap\Gamma_{\operatorname{gen}(b_w)+1}$. This intersection consists of four edges meeting at $b_w$, so every child satisfies the same induction hypotheses, with $m_{wi}=\operatorname{gen}(b_w)$.

The collection of all subtiles $X_{wi}$ obtained above is denoted by $\mathbf X^{n+1}$. The preceding induction together with Lemma \ref{lem: properties of branches of J(f)}(iii) shows that Properties (1)--(4) and the intersection assertion in (5) of Proposition \ref{prop: uniformization of T^4} hold. Moreover, at each subdivision the generation of the newer boundary point increases by at least one and at most three. Consequently, if $w\in A_4^n$, then   $ n-1\leq m_w\leq3(n-1),$ and the subdivision point $b_w$ belongs to $f^{-m}(0)$ for some $n\leq m\leq3n$.

We next associate a core $I_X$ to each tile $X\in\mathbf X^*$. If $\partial X=\{p_X,q_X\}$, set $I_X=J(f)[p_X,q_X].$ If $\partial X=\{b_X\}$, where $b\in E_{X,j_X}$ and  $\operatorname{gen}(b)=m_X$, let  $ I_X=X\cap\Gamma_{m_X+j_X}$. Thus, $I_X=J(f)[b,a]$, where
$a\in f^{-(m_X+j_X)}(\{-q,q\})\cup \{-q,q\}$ is the other endpoint of this edge.

We now describe the finitely many normalized models. For distinct $a,b\in J(f)$, let $B_a(b)$ denote the component of $J(f)\setminus\{a\}$ containing $b$, and, for $t\in\{c,-p,p\}$, set  $P_t=\overline{B_0(t)\cap B_t(0)}.$ In addition, let $P_1,\ldots,P_4$ be the closures of the four components of $J(f)\setminus\{0\}$. We equip $P_t$ with the core $J(f)[0,t]$, and each $P_i$ with the corresponding edge of $\Gamma_1$.

Let $X\in\mathbf X^*$ and write $m=m_X$. Recall that $0\in J(f)[z,-z]$ for every $z\in J(f)$. Thus $f$ is injective on any connected subset of $J(f)$ on which $0$ is absent or is a leaf. Applying this observation successively proves that $f^m|_X$ is injective: at each step, a preimage of $0$ in $X$, if present, is a boundary point and hence a leaf of $X$. The image of $X\setminus\partial X$ is relatively open in $J(f)$, since $f^m$ is locally a homeomorphism there. It is also closed relative to the component determined by the images of the boundary points, by compactness of $X$. Consequently, $f^m$ maps $X$ homeomorphically onto the entire corresponding model and maps $I_X$ onto its prescribed core.

We shall use the following continuation observation for the regular models. Set $S=\{c,p,-p\}$, which contains all finite critical values of every iterate of $f$. For each fixed model $P$, choose a sufficiently thin neighborhood $U_P$ such that $U_P\cap S=P\cap S$. A normalized inverse branch which is regular along $P$ can be analytically continued on $U_P\setminus S$. Since $P$ is a tree, the fundamental group of $U_P\setminus S$ is generated by small loops around the points of $P\cap S$. At each such point the chosen inverse branch has a regular holomorphic germ, so continuation around these loops has trivial monodromy. Hence the branch extends univalently to $U_P$.

We claim that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:core-comparison}
    \operatorname{diam} I_X\simeq\operatorname{diam} X
\end{equation}
for every $X\in\mathbf X^*$. First suppose that $\operatorname{card}(\partial X)=1$. The corresponding inverse branch is regular on the whole normalized model and, by the preceding observation, extends univalently to a fixed neighborhood of it. Since there are only finitely many models and each prescribed core has diameter comparable to that of its model, Lemma \ref{lem:koebe}(i) gives \eqref{eq:core-comparison}. It remains to consider a tile $X$ with $\operatorname{card}(\partial X)=2$. Let $b$ be its older boundary point and put $t=f^m(b)\in\{c,-p,p\}$. The point $b$ is the only critical point of $f^m$ in $X$, and its local degree is two. Thus, if $g=(f^m|_X)^{-1}$, then $g$ has square-root behavior at $t$. Choose $\epsilon_t\in\{-1,1\}$ so that $\epsilon_t(0-t)>0$ and put $\pi_t(\zeta)=t+\epsilon_t\zeta^2$. The model $P_t$ does not meet the ray issuing from $t$ in the direction opposite to its core. Hence the branch $\zeta=\sqrt{\varepsilon_t(z-t)}$ is well defined on $P_t\setminus\{t\}$ with values in the closed right half-plane and extends continuously by sending $t$ to $0$. Let $Q_t$ be the resulting lift of $P_t$, and let $J_t$ be the lift of its core. Define $h_X=g\circ\pi_t.$ The quadratic change of variables removes the criticality at $b$; in particular, $h_X$ extends holomorphically across $0$ with $h_X'(0)\neq0$.

It remains to obtain a uniform neighborhood on which $h_X$ is univalent. Let $S_t=\pi_t^{-1}(S)$. The possible branch values of the relation $f^m(h_X(\zeta))=\pi_t(\zeta)$ belong to the fixed finite set $S_t$. Choose a sufficiently thin bounded simply connected neighborhood $U_t$ of $Q_t$ such that $U_t\cap S_t=Q_t\cap S_t$, and such that $U_t\cap(-U_t)$ is a small disk about $0$ containing no other point of $S_t$. The branch $h_X$ can be analytically continued on $U_t\setminus S_t$. At every point of $Q_t\cap S_t$ it has a regular holomorphic germ, including $\zeta=0$ by the preceding quadratic change of coordinates. Since $Q_t$ is a tree, similar arguments with the regular models show that $h_X$ has a single-valued holomorphic extension to $U_t$. This extension is univalent. Indeed, if $h_X(\zeta)=h_X(\xi)$, then $\pi_t(\zeta)=\pi_t(\xi)$, and hence $\xi=\zeta$ or $\xi=-\zeta$. Put $D=U_t\cap(-U_t)$. If $h_X(\zeta)=h_X(-\zeta)$ for some $\zeta\in D\setminus\{0\}$, differentiation of $f^m(h_X(\zeta))=\pi_t(\zeta)$ gives $(f^m)'(h_X(\zeta))h_X'(\zeta)=2\epsilon_t\zeta\neq0$. Local uniqueness of the inverse therefore implies that the two holomorphic functions $h_X(z)$ and $h_X(-z)$ agree near $\zeta$. By the identity theorem they agree throughout $D$, contradicting $h_X'(0)\neq0$. Hence $h_X$ is univalent on $U_t$. Since there are only three possible values of $t$, the neighborhoods and Koebe constants may be chosen uniformly. Applying Lemma 9.5 on a fixed relatively compact neighborhood of $Q_t$ in $U_t$ gives
\[ \operatorname{diam} h_X(J_t) \simeq \operatorname{diam} h_X(Q_t). \]
Since $h_X(J_t)=I_X$ and $h_X(Q_t)=X$, this proves \eqref{eq:core-comparison}.

The same coordinate estimates give the following scale estimates at the boundary points of a tile. If $b$ is the newer boundary point of $X$, then
\[\operatorname{diam} X\simeq |(f^{m_X})'(b)|^{-1}.\]
For a two-boundary tile this follows from the square-root chart, since the newer boundary point corresponds to one of finitely many fixed nonzero points of $Q_t$, where the change to the ordinary coordinate has nonzero derivative. If $b$ is the older boundary point of a two-boundary tile, then
\[\operatorname{diam} X\simeq |(f^{m_X})''(b)|^{-1/2}.\]

We now prove (5). Let $X,Y\in\mathbf X^n$ be distinct intersecting tiles and write $X\cap Y=\{b\}$. By the construction, $|m_X-m_Y|\leq3$. Indeed, after the first subdivision retaining $b$, the normalization offset from $\operatorname{gen}(b)$ is one of $1,2,3$. Subsequently, $b$ is the older boundary point. In the models $P_c$, $P_p$, and $P_{-p}$, the first interior precritical points have generations $1,2,2$, respectively. Since $f(c)=p$ and $f(p)=f(-p)=-p$, the possible offsets are $1,2,4,6,\ldots$, $2,4,6,8,\ldots$, and $3,5,7,9,\ldots$.

If $b$ is the newer boundary point of $X$ and $Y$, then $m_X=m_Y=\operatorname{gen}(b)$, and the first scale estimate gives immediately
\[\operatorname{diam} X\simeq |(f^{m_X})'(b)|^{-1} = |(f^{m_Y})'(b)|^{-1} \simeq\operatorname{diam} Y.\]

Suppose now that $b$ is the older boundary point of both tiles. Assume, without loss of generality, that $m_Y=m_X+j$, where $0\leq j\leq3$, and put $t=f^{m_X}(b)\in\{c,-p,p\}$. Since $(f^{m_X})'(b)=0$, the chain rule gives
\[(f^{m_Y})''(b) =(f^j)'(t)(f^{m_X})''(b).\]
The forward orbits of $c,p,-p$ avoid $0$, and there are only finitely many possibilities for $t$ and $j$. Hence $|(f^j)'(t)|$ is bounded above and below by positive constants. Using the second scale estimate,
\[ \operatorname{diam} X \simeq |(f^{m_X})''(b)|^{-1/2} \simeq |(f^{m_Y})''(b)|^{-1/2} \simeq\operatorname{diam} Y.\]

For (6), let $X,Y\in\mathbf X^n$ be disjoint, and choose $x\in X$ and $y\in Y$ such that $|x-y|=\operatorname{dist}(X,Y)$. We may assume that $\operatorname{diam} X\geq \operatorname{diam} Y$. The subdivision structure shows that the arc $J(f)[x,y]$ traverses an intermediate tile $Z\in\mathbf X^n$ meeting $X$, entering and leaving $Z$ at two distinct boundary points. Thus $Z$ is an arc tile and $I_Z\subset J(f)[x,y]$. By the core estimate, (5), and bounded turning of $J(f)$,
\[\operatorname{diam} X\simeq\operatorname{diam} Z\simeq\operatorname{diam} I_Z \leq\operatorname{diam} J(f)[x,y] \lesssim |x-y| =\operatorname{dist}(X,Y).\]

For (7), suppose that $X\in\mathbf X^n$ and $Y\in\mathbf X^{n+1}$ with $Y\subset X$. Normalize $X$ to one of the finitely many models. By construction, the subdivision rule is preserved under this normalization: the point $b_X$ is mapped to the first branch point of the corresponding model, and $Y$ is mapped to one of the four subtrees obtained by subdividing that model at this point. Thus, in the ordinary or square-root coordinates introduced above, there is a fixed model $P$ and one of finitely many nondegenerate submodels $P'\subset P$ such that $X=h(P)$ and $Y=h(P')$ where $h$ is univalent on a fixed neighborhood of $P$. Since there are only finitely many possible pairs $(P,P')$,  $\operatorname{diam} P'\simeq \operatorname{diam} P.$ The uniform Koebe estimates therefore give
\[\operatorname{diam} Y=\operatorname{diam} h(P')\simeq \operatorname{diam} h(P)=\operatorname{diam} X.\]
The case $X=J(f)$ is absorbed by enlarging the constant. Last, if $Y\not\subset X$, then it follows from $(5)$ and the same above argument.

Finally, we verify (8). By Lemma \ref{lem:koebe}(ii) and the finite-model estimates above, there exist $C_1\geq1$ and $\rho\in(0,1)$ such that, whenever $X\in\mathbf X^n$ and $Y\in\mathbf X^{n+k}$ with $Y\subset X$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:descendant-shrinking}
    \operatorname{diam} Y\leq C_1\rho^k\operatorname{diam} X .
\end{equation}
To see this, first note that if $f^\ell$ maps a subtree $Q$ homeomorphically onto a normalized model $R$, then  $ \operatorname{diam} Q\lesssim\lambda^\ell.$ Indeed, since there are only finitely many models, each $R$ can be covered by at most $L$ compact subtrees $K_1,\ldots,K_L$, where $K_i\subset B(z_i,r)$ and $L$ is uniform. The sets $(f^\ell|_Q)^{-1}(K_i)$ are connected and lie in components of $f^{-\ell}(B(z_i,r))$. Hence Lemma\ref{lem:koebe2} gives diameter at most $C\lambda^\ell$ for each of them. Since they cover the connected set $Q$, their intersection graph is connected, and therefore     $\operatorname{diam} Q\leq LC\lambda^\ell.$ Thus, if $Q=f^{m_X}(Y)$ and $\ell=m_Y-m_X$, then $k\leq\ell\leq3k$ and $f^\ell$ maps $Q$ homeomorphically onto one of the finitely many normalized models. Exponential shrinking gives $\operatorname{diam} Q\lesssim\lambda^k$. For tiles with one boundary point the coordinate estimates preserve this bound, while for tiles with two boundary points the square-root coordinate gives $\operatorname{diam} Y/\operatorname{diam} X\lesssim_f\lambda^{k/2}$. Thus \eqref{eq:descendant-shrinking} holds with $\rho=\sqrt{\lambda}$. After increasing $C_1$, the same estimate also holds when $X=J(f)$.

Let $C_0\geq1$ be the comparability constant in (5), and choose $N\in\mathbb N$ sufficiently large that $2C_0C_1\rho^N\leq 1$. Suppose that $X\in\mathbf X^n$ and $Y\in\mathbf X^m$ satisfy $m\geq n+N$ and $X\cap Y\neq\emptyset$. Let $X'\in\mathbf X^n$ be the ancestor of $Y$. Then $X\cap X'\neq\emptyset$, and hence by (5), $\operatorname{diam} X'\leq C_0\operatorname{diam} X$. Applying \eqref{eq:descendant-shrinking} to $Y\subset X'$ gives
\[\operatorname{diam} Y \leq C_1\rho^{m-n}\operatorname{diam} X' \leq C_0C_1\rho^N\operatorname{diam} X \leq\frac12\operatorname{diam} X.\]
Enlarging the common constant in (5)–(8) to be at least $N$ proves (8).
<!-- end proof-37 -->
